PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

MANY a sweetheart has a sour disposition. It takes some worms a terribly long time to turn. _ _

' AFTER a man discovers how little he knows, he begias to suspect that possibly others do not know as much as they pretend. TIIE QUESTION.—Mrs. A.: "I bought a lot of things at the bargain sale yesterday." Mrs. B. : "Did you get anything you wanted ?" A THEORY.— Josh: a wonder none of them fellers ever found the North Pole." Hiram: " Mebbe it ain't there." EVERY INDICATION.—First Actor: "Was the piece well done?" Second Actor: "I should say so. The critics roasted it.'

_ A DMIT occasionally to your friend that he knows more than yr.o do. It gives him a great hn- pression of your discrimination and good sense.

ONE evening a man was performing the old trick of producing eggs from a pocket-handkerchief, when he remarked to a little boy in front—" Say, ray boy, your mother can't get eggs without hens, can she ? "Of course Phe can,', said the boy. "Why, how is that?" asked the conjuror. "She keeps ducks." "How do I know that you really love me ?" she asked. "What assurance have I that you would be willing to make sacrifices and endure hardships for my sake ? " He looked at her in reproachful astonishment, and exclaimed—" What more can you ask? Haven't I for six months refrained from laying violent hands on your _little brother ? "

LADIES' LETTER.I

MY DE4R LLIE, Very pretty dresses can be made for little girls, and though cheap they may be very endurable. I eaw a .little girl of the age of about 10 at a small party the ether day who wore the popular "sailor " shape dress. The material was blue, of course, the appearance of the dress being considerably im- vroved by the smart way the trimming had been stitched on the large white collar. The advantage of these dresses is that very often they can be made at home by the child's clever elder sister or mother. In the case under notice the garment was home- made and certainly looked extremely becoming.

The most casual

have noticed that

autumn toques ate very various in shape and trimming. Every variety of face, whether broad or long, thin or fat, small-featured or the reverse, can be comfortably suited without passing beyond the limits of the fashions of the hour. A toque that is worn well back on the head, and would suit a low forehead, is made of plum-coloured velvet with a gauged crown and fully pleated brim, turned up at the left side with a large cluster of shaded chrysanthemums and a black feather, the tip of which falls over the brim and rests on the hair. A handsome hat is in black chip, covered with pale blue chiffon, closely pleated in flat folds and sewn with rows of black chenille. At the left side is a wired band in blue velvet, a black plume, and a steel buckle. This hat would look well with white chenille or black chiffon for half-mourning wear; or in stii-phed black silk.

The high collar has reappeared, but, in my opinion, under conditions that greatly alter it for the better. It no longer closely encloses the throat, enfeebling it and rendering it readily sus- ceptible of cold. Though it rises high at the back, it can easily be folded back, and is therefore not nearly so harmful as it was in previous years. Fewer people wear it now than was the case two or three seasons since. It is more generally recog- nised that the line of the neck and shoulder is too important to the general outline to be unneces- sarily hidden away. For the same reason, the boa is now universally left open in front, either to hang with long strands of chenille as ballast on each side, or else it is pinned at the waist in a diagonal line from the shoulder. This plan aids the chief feature of dress at the moment—viz., the em- bellishment of the front of the bodice by various means.

' The ideal of modern dressmakers and tailors is a long waist, whether in proportion or out of pro- portion to the length of the limbs. Every device is employed to lengthen the waist, from the pres- sure administered by means of the stiffly-boned corset, down to the skilful manipulation of material by the ladies' tailor. In making riding-habits this is more particularly the object of the man- milliner, and the consequence is that many a fair rider who looks splendid on horseback turns out to be a squat little creature when she withdraws her foot from the stirrup. It would be truer art to endeavour to preserve some kind of proportion between the dimensions of the trunk and the limbs. When the latter are very short it is always a mistake to let the bodice overlap the skirt, whether in basques or merely in points that over- hang the belt. The ties that are all the rage are those of black satin or velvet, coming from a stock collar of gold braid or stitched velvet, the two ends crossed on the chest and passed through a quaint clasp, while sack end is gathered into tips of enamel, or filigree gold set with jewels. Fussy bows on cravats are no longer the mode, but I saw a most elegant scarf of silk, the ends of richest floss silk em- broidery, fringed deeply. Diamond rings clasped the scarf here and there, and the collarband was sewn with tiny diamonds.

The styles o'f evening dress are simple, but most elegant. A white and green dress, fresh and dainty, was in waved lines of satin, bordered wiLn crystals, while festoons of white tulle, sprinkled with crystals, separated .the stripes ; at the back, a little to one side, was a green tulle sash, but, instead of flowing loose, the bows and loops were stitched down here and there to the skirt, which appears to be a novel idea on many evening gowns. Black and white is well worn for evening, and for day wear as well. A gown of peculiar charm was a black and white check, of the new velvet cloth, the skirt cut into deep pointed tabs, which were stitched down to an under-petticoat of a series of black cloth strips, each strip left with raw cut edges. The bodice was very plain and tight- fitting. Your loving .I.IIVNITTA

THE MOST NUTRITIOUS.

CHRISTMAS CARDS.

Messrs. Raphael Tuck and,4Bons, are again early in the field with their magnificent assortment of Christmas cards, booklets, calendars, and the like. There is the saute spirit of artistic invention and originality evident in this season's goods from Raphael House that has long been a feature of the past. Messrs. Tuck have not overlooked the ten- dency of the times and there is a strong military leavening of the Christmas cards. As they have long possessed the kingship of the Christmas and New Year card trade, the firm now aspire to a still loftier pinnacle and have begun to sigh, they tell us, for en imperial diadem and are mak- ing a bold bid" for an empire in the world of art. Probably they will attain the height of their ambition, andi if 1,200 new sets of cards embodying 3,000 individual de- signs are any criterion they have won the title to sway an empire already. The principal card of the collection, is this season, the "gem pendant," a graceful double folding card of entirely new shape, which well lends itself to artistic treatment. In addition there are the "Pompeian," the "Patriotic," the "Art Bronze," the " Wedgwood." and the "Oak platino panels." All are quite novel and distinctly beautiful, but the last mentioned strikes us as being a particularly pleasing idea. Platinoe were never made to look nicer than they de mounted 'on the imitation oak which Messrs. Tuck have designed, and platinos are among our favourites. We are somewhat sorry that the number of black and whites generally seems to have decreased ; they are always among the most tasteful and acceptable creations. There is a choice, hough small. selectioa of them however. Calen- dars are, generally speaking, losing the pride of place which they may be said to have once occupied, and some of them this year are hardly up to Messrs Tuck's standard either in originality or beauty. Humorous cards are a strong line, the military spirit being responsible for a lot of red-coat humour, and they will we think be voted "the very thing." Juvenile toy and gift books are also full of merit, and the same may be said of the art novelties which have been designed to please the youthful eye and mind. As in previous years we can still confidently recommend all purchasers of this class of goods to" plump for Tuck who has represented them in the (Raphael) House with conspicuous abiii ity and may be relied upon to more than fulfil his promises in the future.

THE HOUSE BY THE RIVER.

BY MRS. C. N. WILLIAMSON, Author of "The Secret 'of the Pearls," "The Home by the Lock, "Queen Swgetheart," "Th. Barnstormers,' "Ifer Royal Ifighness," Etc.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Lancelot Wade, a well-connected ne'er-do-well, lives with his daughter Sybil in fourth-floor rooms in Blooms- bury. One day the fattier brings Sybil the extra- ordinary news that she—she who never went anywhere or saw anybody in her life—is to go to a dinner- parry at Lady Northniuir's house in Berkeley-square, where she meets a certain JPC:( Churchill, who is exi e ted by Lady Carstairs, another guest, to marry her daughter Gladys. The dinner, like the house, Is very grand, and Sybil is sui remely uncomfortable. Next morning Lancelot Wade does not appear at breakfast; Sybil finds that 1,i3 bed has not been slept in. Alarmed, she is about to go to the police- station, when a messenger-boy appears with a letter from her father. The envelope is hold-smeared, and the mess nger hesitatingly aunts that lie thinks the'', has been an accident. Sybil is con- ducted by Vie messenger-bay, by me ns of trams and trains, quite out of the city to a lonely house on the river bank. She is told to enter a cemiia room, and, expecting to see her father, she un- he‘itatingly enters. The to im is errptv ! Anil Sybil, when she tries to get out. again, finds herself a prisoner. Meanwhile Churchill, on his way to the birthday-feast of Gladys, reads of Lancelot Wade' suicide, and carries/. the news to Lady Carstairs. Churchill does not, know that Sybil has disappearei, and Lady Carstairs seeks to quench his interest in her by lying to film about the way she is being cared for and the low marriage she is about to contract. Lady Carstairs herself at this tune does not know v hat has become of Sybil and she seeks the assistance of her son, Lord Carstairs, to get her put out of the way so that Churchill shall never see her and dis- cover the lies told about her. It is very evident that Lord Carstairs has a powerful private motive for getting rid of Sybil, though his mother knows nothing of it as yet. He it was, indeed, who arranged for Sybil's capture, the intention being to murder her as her father was murdered. In her extremity, however, Sybil is assisted to escape by Hester Frayne, the girl who, disguised as a mes- senger lad, had decoyed her from home.

trol of her voice, she won't' question nester Frayne about the:letter. She remembered very well bow the supposed messenger-boy had stammered and cast down his eyes when she had exclaimed in terror at sight of the blood-stain on the paper. A plot to get rid of her and her father seemed so futile, so inexplicable; and yet, viewed from the point at which she now stood, how easy of accom- plishment it appeared The landlady at 20, Mitchell-street would only believe that they had run away to escape the obligation of paying their debts. Sybil could see that it would be hard indeed to persuade her otherwise. The woman would think that Sybil's alarm at her father's absence was assumed; or, if not, that, at all events, he had aommunicated with her, and bidden her join him— bidding which she had promptly obeyed. Co far as Sybil knew there was no one on earth who cared enough for poor Lancelot Wade and his daughter to make any effort towards ascertaining their fate if they suddenly vanished. Lady North- muir would take it for granted that they had chosen to leave London without letting her know. Lord Carstairs and his mother and sister would be relieved that their inconvenient poor relations had departed without giving them any more trouble. As for Jack Churchill, she had told herself before this that, despite his kindness and the look in his eyes, which seethed to mean interest, he would have forgotten all alout Gladys Wade's little cousin in a day or two. "So my father and I could be put out of the world without danger that our disappearance would disturb anyone," the girl thought. "Somebody must have known that and counted upon it—known what a roving life we led, so that none of our acquaintances could ever tell where we would be -the day after they had seen us last. Somebody must have very carefully planned all this. But who—who? No one but a maniac would commit crimes without reason—without desire for revenge or the hope of advantage to himself." The shock of hearing that her father was dead merged into the enthralling excitement of specula- tion. She turned to Hester Frayne and spoke with a firm and steady voice. "Who is the mister of this house? Tell me that and I will try to ask-you very few more questions." "That, of all things, I will not tell—now, or ever!" answered the Other. "I meant all along to make you promise, before I took you away to safety—if I should suctceed—that you would not even try to find out where was the house you have been in to-day, or who was the man by whose orders you were brought here. Ido not love that man any more. I said to you a little while ago that I hated him; but I don't hate him enough to wish that he shouldobe ruined." "How can I make such a promise," cried Sybil, "when I know very well—though you have not admitted it—that the man is my father's murderer? If I escape, am I to be silent and leave my father unavenged ? " "I thought that you were probably a Christian," retorted Hester Frayne. "Vengeance is mine, saith the Lord.' Would you not be ready to obey those words? 'k+

"How could I be so selfish as to accept my own safety as a bribe for leaving a murderer undis- covered ? "

* Yon have forgotten that I have compromised myself for ever with my only friends because of you," answered Hester. "You have me to think of now, as well as yourself, if you have the good heart I've given you credit for. I can't help you to leave this house unless you promise me what I have asked; and I have gone too far to draw back -,,rw and let things. be as they were before with me. we stay here, I shall have to die, as well as you; remember that !"

"I promise, then," returned Sybil, desperately. "You leave me no choice except to promise."

"You swear that, if I can get you safely away, you will be silent as to what has passed here—ypu will make no complaint to the police, but be satisfied with having (swiped? You swear that you will try to forget this hateful day and night; or, at all events, to live as if you had dreamt them, as if this house, and those who brought and kept you here, only existed in the dream ? " "Yes, I suppose I must do as you ask," Sybil said, slowly, and with hesitation. I'l swear that I will do nothing to revenge myself or my father if I escape. Yet, tell me this one thing. If my father died of apoplexy, as you say, • how came that blood on the letter he wrote to me ?"

"I don't know. As truly as I live I don't know," answered Hester. "I may guess, but my guesses are worth nothing. I had promised to perform the errand to you—promised only half-an-hour before the man you say was your father came to this house; and when the messenger-boy's livery was got for me—there was some delay about that—l went without daring to ask questions, except about the way to go, and exactly by what roundabout routes I was to bring you back. But, now that I am sure of you—for, you see, I trust your word— we must begin to think how we are to get away. "The man I have spoken of is surely out search- %Lig for us now, thinking cruel, revengeful things of me for thwarting him. My stepmother—who was glad to help him, and to have me help him,. in the hope that I should be his wife—is waiting for him to Come back with news. By this time howill, perhaps, be far away from the house, riding the horse he keeps here at- the stables, .perhaps, hopinS to overtake us. But she—if I only knew where she was ; f could be half-sure that she would not spy us as we stole away !"• "Even if she did see us," argued Sybil, "you say that she is lame. We might riin by, and she could not catch us if there is nobody else in the 'liaise whom she could send in pursuit.

"Yes, we might do that,ik". repined Hester, doubtfully. "But the man isTound to come back discouraged before very, long, and when he heard that we sad. so lately been here, that we had got so very little start, he would go out again and would be almost sure to find us."

"At least, we should be out of this house. We could scream and call for help if he tried to harm us."

"It is very lonely in this neighbourhood," answered Hester. "We should have to go a very long way before we could do any mood by crying for help. And—and he would be desperate. I think if we were found he . would never let us go alive. I am afraid of him—oh, I am afraid of everything. I have gone through so much to-day, risked so much, that the reaction has come: I feel if it would be better to stay here in the attic till to-morrow night if we could bear it. Then the man would have gone away, and we should have al better chance of escape. They say people can livel a long time without food if there is a real necessity. Are you very hungry now?" "I was hungry, but now I am only faint and sick," said Sybil. "I had a cup of tea and half a slice of toast for breakfast this morning; it sepms hundreds of years ago. But I think I forgot to' eat the toast, as already I was so anxious about my poor father. And I have had nothing since." "I don't believe you could live, then, till to- morrow night," replied Hester, thoughtfully. "You are only a delicate little thing, and you must be nearly fainting now. Whatever happens, I.can't let you die here in this attic among the rats. Don't you hear their little feet and their sharp, horrid little teeth gnawing the wood, even now? We must take our chances to-night, after all. Have you the courage?" "Yes," said Sybil. "I would rather face death openly than hide here in the dark, not knowing each moment what will come with the next." "I believe that you are braver than I," the other girl exclaimed. 'I seem spent. I can hardly think what is best to do. Anyway, I shall light our candle again. Perhaps, when we are not in this choking darkness, I shall get my wits and nerve again." She struck a match, and presently the small flame the candle flickered, rose, grew stendy, and made the curtain of blackness hanging round them seem the more distinctly visible. Hester gave a deep sigh, looked wistfully round her as if for some help out of the.great danger she fecred, and took up the candle, which had been welded to the floor by its own grease. "We'll light oprselves down the stairs, and at the bottom put out the candle again," she said. If we must go—and we must, I know—it will be better now -while the man is sure to be away, than later." _ _ _

As they rose and tiptoed out of their .secluded corner behind the furniture, the sound of„their light footsteps and rustling dresses stopped the gnawing orthe rats, which had grown acotstomed to the soft, whispering voices. The retreat which the two girls had chosen was well out of sight from thl stairs, which, rounding a juttinq projection of bricked chimney, they came upon suudenly. Hester held the caaulle, drops of hot grease dripping over her fingers; but Sybil was by her side, and simultaneously they became aware that they were not alone in the attic. A taft woman, carrying a lamp, had come noislessly up the stairs, and stood' now at the' top starlit," towards the ggures that advanced with the mild.. A ',TAME OF FIVE LETTEEP.

"Somebody offered you money?" ejaculated Sybil, chilled by her creeping amazement that anyone on earth could have thought her undoing worth a tempting sum of money. "No ; not that !" hastily rejoined llester Frayne. "I'm not going to defend myself. But suppose. you had loved a man, and looked up to him as a kind of prince, belonging to a world as far above you, almost as heaven is above earth, and he asked you to do something for him, wouldn't you go to almost any lengths for the sake of doing it ?" For a moment Sybil hesitnted before answering. She was not yet eighteen, and she had never been in love; but as Hester Frayne put the question, in a passionate, tremulous whisper, a strange picture seemed to rise before her eyes. She could see Jack Churchill's face—not merry and smiling, or gravely earnest, as it had looked during their talk together at Lady Northmuir's, but pleading, with a wonderful light in the dark eyes, such, as she knew, they were capable of holding. lie was asking a boon of her, and offering infinite love in exchange.

Suddenly, as the vision faded, her heart began to throb almost to suffocation.

"Yes—yes!" she exclaimed. "I would give my right hand to serve a man, if I loved him, and he loved me ! But—but--"

" Ah, you are going to say you wouldn't do any- thing wicked, even if he a.ked it of you. Yet listen to me. I have loved a man—all my life I have loved him, tint il to-night. For I think I hate him now. Once he told me that he cared for me, but he had a great position to keep up, and he must marry a woman in his own sphere of life—a woman who had money and a place in society like his own. So there was no happiness for me ; I must forget him, if 1 could, lie had made me care ; yet I didn't think of that—l didn't blame him for that then. I was only dreadfully, dread- fully unhappy. At last he said there was a service I could do for him—a service so great that there was but one reward good enough, if it was done, and succeeded. This 'reward was that I should be his wife. Without even Avaiting to ask what the service was to be, I promised. And then —then something happened here, in this house, that made me afraid of the promise." The girl's voice faltered. She was speaking now more as if to herself than to Sybil, whose presence for a moment she had forgotten. "What do you mean ? What happened here ?" cried Sybil. "Was it anything about my father? Was he really here, after all?

As she spoke, she could feel a sudden shudder that quivered through the slim form of the girl crouching close to her side—a shudder that shook it from head to foot. No answer came. "I must know! You must tell me if iny father has been in this house !" Sybil persisted, a ring of determination in her young voice. _

"A man was here, ihen, 7if you will know. I !can't tell you if he was your father or not. I did not hear his name. But lie was one of the hand- somest men I ever saw, though he was no longer very young. He must have been forty at least. He had dark eyes, and hair just touched with grey; a moustache that was darker than his hair; and his chin seemed to be cleft in two by a great dimple. It was late last night that he came—all alone. I was sitting up, and I opened the door for him."

"It was my father. You have described him 'exactly I" Sybil exclaimed. "What happened to him in this dreadful house? Did they. murder him, as you say they will try to murder me?" I "I don't exactly, know what happened. He came, and there were some angry words between him and another—the master of this house. The man was very much excited, and I was told that he had had a stroke of apoplexy. He was dead long before I was sent with the letter to you."

"Dead? My father dead?" Sybil faltered. She had feared many things, but until now she had had hope. • • "Yes.; he, is dead. I knew that, yet I had to take the letter to you which was to bring you here. If I had refused to keep my promise someone else would have gone; and, if they meant to harm you I should not then be able to do anything to prevent it. At least, I think I said that to my conscience beforel went; but perhaps the truth still was that I could not bear to go against the man I loved, wicked .as he might be. I wouldn't let myself believe the worst of him. I said: 4 Whatever he may be, at least he loves me, and will make me his wife.' Since then, through an accident, I have found out that he never cared—that he meant to use me as a cat's-paw to draw the chestnuts he wanted for himself out of the fire, so that his hands need not be burnt; then he. intended to throw me over—whistle Me down the wind. He knew that I would be too deep in the affair to dare betray him to the police, even if I were spiteful enough to wish it. So you see, afber all, it was more selfishness than repentance that has made me help you. Only, when I first saw your face, and found that you were so young, so sweet, so beautiful, my heart grew sick. I could not say to myself anrmore, as he had said to me: Oh, no harm will come to her!' I was afraid for you, and I was more miserable than I had ever been in my life. Yet, if { hadn't found out that he was deceiving me all along, it is just possible I might have Shut my eyes to any- 'thing that was going on, and tried to fancy I really believed afterwards anything they chose to tell me about your disappearance." "My disappearance ? " echoed Sybil, shivering. "I think that, if I had left you in that room down below, you would have disappeared before morning." Sybil drew in her breath sharply. but did not sPeak. Never, until to-day, had there been any- thing suggestive of mystery in her life. Now she found herself stifled with it—gasping for free, unpolluted air. She sat still, thinkinc, intently, feeling deathly cold and faint. She.did' not doubt now that her father had been murdered 'by some secret enemy of whose existence she had never known, and for a reason she could not guess. Either the letter which this girl—disguised as the slim, smart young im ta mger-boy—had brought her was a forgery, or else her father had, by some violent means, been forced to write it before his death.

The last thought was almo,t too horrible to cott template ; but the stain of red on the paper, now that she knew something of the truth, pointed to some such solution. The letter had been stolen away from her now, and she could not make sure whether the handwriting—only casually examined in the excitement of receiving news—was genuine or 1,.0t. But by-and-bve, when she could regain con-

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By " Ovalist '] Toe Warrington spectators had tho tuxury of a cup-tie on Saturday, and as the reqult of it was a great triumph for the home team they must have appreciated it very highl7 and be now in the sten of Oliver Twist—longing for more. As a rule we don't expect ckip-ti:!s b,fore the end of the season, but the South %Vest Lancashire Cup Competition being now on a proper footing we get them early on. Not only that, but it is j Ist possible that we —Wmington I—may possess a c.fp of our ,own before the N attarrn 11,ion cup ties commence. Stich ikaM3 as Swinton, Runcorn, St. Helens and Leigh, all have a voice in the matter at present however. It will not do to be too exp:lctant and hopeful. I ha Je had lops of Warrington oefore, and ultimatey fouad them rudely dashed to di! ground.

Widnes had the god fortune to bs drawn at War- ington for the opening of the competition and are now in the happy position of being rslieved of all fur her anxiety with regard to the destination of the cup. Th y had some notion, I am told, of retrieving their fallen fortunes at WarrinAten'a expense and therefore brouht their strongest tram, which at preeeut consists of eit-it rattling good forwards. six "A " team and one first team back. The latter is Baty who shines all by him- self as a sort of wandering Jew with a mission te do as much damag,e to the other sids as psssible. He doss no do much 'tis true, but that is nt for wa:.t of tryinz. Any way he is the solo survivor of last season's back division of the Widnes' team. Warrington only lamented th,, absence of one back, J T Bite, but in the front rat,k there were no less wan four chinges necessary. Thu, things seemed to be about ecrially balanced at the com- mencement. for wherias Widnes were expected to have the pull forward. Warringloa should be stronser behind. The prediction was not far wrong, teceept th it the pull forward for the visitors was not maintain and even- tually the home backs ran riot and tore the Widnes de- fence to small pieces.

Neverthelesa there was a time when the prospPct looked ominous for the hems I.,cam. This was in tie firs: five nainue,. for Widriei mide a rapid rail and sCOnti a try lustore Warrinco, hid proprly got their eyfs open. The reverse act, d 113 a cold douche and five minutos liter Warrington wer, nn level terms as the outcome of a spirited attack. Fish was the scorer. Then Widnes diA another spasmodic bust and were two points ahe id a ;Ain as the recoil of a :lin irt drop kick float the foo,. (if LI .1:i 4in• F,41 enable.l Warrinit )a to retaliate, D ck nO.i this tote bein, the s trot' awl to make thifus doubly secure Fisi ran in anoth,q• tr.s, him- self. Tnen Widop‘s micle a d;iog spurt and obtained another two pJial9.

There was a spell of real cup--,ie vigour in the Second half, when Widnes mtde a determined effort to secure the mastery. Their chance was gone, however, for Warrington appeared to improve both in dash and com- bination. Three times was the vistors' line crossed,once by Fish and twice by the forwards, who playe i an uncommonly smut game a,.; this stage. Fish also kicked a goal, and Widnes being run to a standstill, the final caine with the score 20-7 in favour of the home club.

The result is a very creditabie one for the homester3, and for the first time this season they " rose to the oect- slots." Tne for wards,lighter than ever, knew what was expectei of them, and made efforts which surpasied anything done previously, and quitz turned the scale. ,Jarringtou's back division then had a day out. Fish himelf annexing 11 points, so that his record for th two last matches stands at 2 goals. 5 tries (19 points). It must not be imagined, however, that Warrington proved past form to be no criterion. Bating Widnes by twenty points to seven points does not make them a firw-clasi team, and it was plain that the weakness ot the visiting side had more to do with Warrington's surprising slleCTis than their own ability. There are still innumerable weak spots, and the only places that could not be bettered are full back and left wing.

Two or three forwards possessing bone and muscle of a different standard to that grown lora Warrington will have to be obtained. You can't rely on such reserves as thcal of Saturday for first-class football, and apart from the questiou of reserves the club at no trnie this season have possessed enough men to fill the forward positions creditably. We have five good forwards.at the most—the others want importing. And I am almost tired of reiterating the fact that a couple of three. quarters are required to make the middle line effective. Perhaps they will come soon—in Father Christmas's stocking may-be.

Warrington have a very weak team this season,and if Fish did not score so often and Hallam so freqnently pre- vent the olier side from sooting. we should have been at the bottom of the League by now.

ReZerring once again to last Satureay's match I must again compliment the two men just mentioned. They played a superb game and quite delighted the crowd with the finished exhibition they gave. Isherwood showed snne of his old form in defensive play but has not recovered the knack of getting throuh and making openings. It will come back to him no doubt. Hocken- hall was a complete failure. For some weeks he has been deteriorating and los; all title to his place by Saturday's display. Duckworth filled Bate's place very creditably, and in fact both half backs passed out better than any time before. The three-quarters were thus kept on the move and though not a class lot, they com- bined in a fashion which made the result a certainty for warringtou. Dickenson this time justified his selection and played a very promising game, though the char- acter of the opposing centres must not be forgotten. Kinsella was the pick of the Widnes team and gave an exhibition at full back which did him infinite credit. Baty was just as big a failure and seemed unable even to get out of the way when not waived. Aspey was Use most conspicuous man in a set of forwards who soon lost heart.

A Local "Forward _Movement"

_ Anew movement for the improvement of the morals of the poorer classes was initiated by Mr. William Nightingale and an enthusiastic band of working men on Sunday evening, when a meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick-street There was an encouraging attendance and a profit- able time was spent.Solos,recitals,&c.,were given in an excellent manner and an address was delivered by Mr. Nightingale in which he explained the ob- jects and needs of the movement. He said it was a temperance, social and religious movement.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, NOVEMBER 9, 1900.

THE HISTORY OF THE RUNCORN

FERRY. From Brindley to the Transporter Bridge. ---- Lecture by Mr. W. Handley.

Nontiatte feoin last Week.]

Tin ferry continued under such conditions for about 350 years, the Barony of Halton, having in the mean- while become merged in the Dachy of Lancaster. In the year 1399, bath Barony and Duchy became united to tnP Crown in th % person of Henry Boliubroke, the 15th Baron and Duke of Lancaster, by his accession to the Throne of England as Henry the' fourth. From that date tee ancient Ferry became vested in the Crown. The Order of the Knights Hospetalera was abolished by Henry VIII, at the general dissolution of the Monasteries and their propirtlea were cenfiscated by the King. The Barony of Haaton from that time has been administered by a seneschal or steward appointed by the Crown as •part of the Duchy of Lancaster. lam reminded from ' the account in the current local paper of the Halton Court Leet. held a ie al days ay.°, that this office is still in existence in the persou of Relent Davies Esq. (Warring- ton), whci has been tor many years the seneschal or steaard a the Queen as Baroness of Halton and Duchess of Lancaster. Ii the reigns of Queens Mary and Eta death this office was vested in Sir John Savage, of Rock Savege, and his heirs ; and, who, for a long series of years wag a faithful and trusted officer, deeervedy held in high favour by his sov,h.eign. It is therefore but a circumstance in aecerdance with the custom of those days to read that the whole of the Manors of Cheshire were in the possession of the S avage tamily,ostensibly as marks of Royal favour. In the manorial grant there was doubtless included the ancient Ferry of Runcorn. Oa the failure of the male line of the Savage family in 1728. the title and estates 'pissed with the distaff to the Cholmendeleys. They es Lords of the Manor, let the ferry on lease at an annual rental, though, up to the opening of the rail- way on the Lancashire side of the river this could not have been more than nominal. In the year 1828 the Duchy of Lancaster caused a survey to be made of the whole of the estuary of tee Mersey. The report which was issued, in th portioe relating to Runcorn, states : " that the Marqaie of Cnolmende ey wal the owner of the ancient ferry, road, and boat house, there being no slip or other accommoelatien provided for passeneers, who were landed on the open beach." In the portion relating to Widnes, it is stated that Mr William Hurst, the owner of the land on the Lancashire shore, also claimed an interest in the ferry and the adjoining marsh ; a right whin the Duchy co itesPed in the lew courts, obtaining a verdict in its favour, but Mr. Hurst appeared to have been granted a vested interest as the report goes en to state" Mr Hurst claims the strand under the West Bank, cuts such rock as be may require for his own use from it, and is paid tor all goods landed and placed there; but lithe goods are carted away immediately, toll for passing over his land only is enacted." The report further states: "The , terry belongs to the Marquis of Cholmondeley, but Mr Hurst and his tenants on the West Bank estate have the right to pass and repass without any payment" From a paragraph in Fowle.'s visitors guide published in 1834 be. is doubtful it a regular terry then existed, as it reed, "Persons may be ferried over the river at any hour of the day by applicatioraat the ferry office near the Baths." A description of being ferried across the river under present conditions would be little more than farcical, but waen Rencern was dependent on the ferry as its almost only way out, crossing the narrow stream proved to be not iefrequently an eventful voyage. A century ago a travel- ler thus described it. "To cross the Mersey near Runcorn by means of the ferry-boat is at any time tedious and inconvenient and in the boisterous weather of the perilous months of winter is teeth difficult and dangerous, and at times impracticable." Another writer a few yea: s later des Iribes it as " little more than an arm of the sea, the tide running throueh its narrow channel with such impetuosity th at at times at was difficult for ves,els to euey :heir rudders." Within tae memory of many yat living, and even subsequent to the onenine of tee railways, a weary and often dangerous avatic journey in the earls' hours of a chilly dark morning nad to be ma to to the Lancashire shore and a walk of some six or seven miles undertaken in order te jin the train at Rainhill for Liverpool or Manchester. Fey •ra at a later date we read in Fowler's Guide "By the Reneorn Gap Railway passengers may be conveyed daily to St. Helens, Manchester. or Liverpool. The train r starts every morning frem the office at Widnes Dock (the Iron Bridge) at seven o'clock, and every after- noon at a quarter'before three o'clock, an] returns from Sc. Helens and Sutton Bridga, bringing passengers from Liverpool and Manchester, at half-past seven o'clock in the morning and at aqu treer past three in the after- noon." Imaeine business or pleasure having to be con- ducted or enjoyed under such limited facilities. So re- cently as the year 1857 the following paragraph which appeared in a local paper can be scarcely read without raising a smile " On Saturday last we took tickets far Widnes %effete is the nearest station to Runcorn. We then had to walk about a mile, through streets the whole of which appeared to be suffering from some chronic distemper, judging from the state of the side waltra and roadway, until tee arrived at the brow of.. the slope leading to the Ferry, Here we had a choice two walks, both of which were in a disgraceful unfinished state, and their proximity induced the belief that the rival roads must be the bone of contention in the Local Board. It appeared to be low water when we wanted to cross. A notice outside the booking office warns. passengers that UnieS3 they leave Runcorn forty- five, minutes before the time at which the train leaves IV id. nes.,the Company will not guarantee that they will be in time. At low water the river runs in two deep streams, one close to each shore, After passing the first stream, passengers are landed oa a wet sandy bank, across which they have to paddle to the edge of the other stream, where the second boat awaits them to land them on the opposite shore." Trio writer adds " This will give some idea of the inconveniences to which the Rencornians are subjected when on their travels." (Laughter.) The last lease of the Ferry was granted by the Marquis of Cholmondeley to Gilbert Greenall, Eq., and others as representing the St. Helens Railway Compansaan extract from which may possess some little interest even at the present day. The indenture recites the leasing for twenty years from the 25th of March. 185, of " all that ferry or passage palled Runcorn Ferry over the River Mersey, as well as in Runcorn in the Cassette of Chester, as in Widnes in the County of Lancaster, with the passage through the ford there in case the same river be fordable, together with the boat hires, tolls, or sums of money for the carriage of passengers, cattle and goods, together with all other rights, members and appurtenances belonging to the Ferry, except and always reserved out of this pre- sent demise the lands called the beat lands, and also the liberty of passage for the said Marquis of Caolmonde- ley, his heirs and assigns, and his or their workmen,and servants, at all times in the terry boats, and for his and their carts and carriages throuih the ford." Both this lease and the interest of the Marquis in the ferry,coupled with the obligation to work and keep it open, were bougbt under Parliamentary Sanction in 1861 by the London and North Western Railway Company immed- iately prior to the commencement of the erection of the Bridge; since which date the Ferry has been supposed to be kept in working order in an efficient and satisfac- tory manner. The ferry boats in use at the time of the transfer were larger and more numerous than the ones at present in use. They were large open barges specially constructed not only for the conveyance of passengers, but also for the carriage of all kinds of goods cattle and merchandise. Thu were fitted with masts and sails and also with oars. The men who navigated them were selected because of their skill and experience of the changing currents of the river, and it was their pride that though the ferrying in boisterous and wintry I weather was hazardouseaccidente were unknown, it was a common occurrence for the Ferry cargo to include sheep,cattle,horaee,general goods,handcartssbarrows,and human passengers ; the latter being often called upon, and always willing to lend a hand at plying an oar in order to ensure a quicker passage. During the latter years of the construction of the Ship Canal, and also for some time subsequently to its opening,the ferry was and remained closed. Fearing that the ancient prescriptive right was in jeopardy the Widnes Corporation and the Runcorn Urban District Council decided to take joint legal action, but this was rendered unnecessary as the Ship Canal and the Railway Company came to an agree- ment and the" Ancient Ferry" was re-opened and the right preserved. The first proposal of which there is any direct evidence was made in the year 1768 by Mr James Brindley whe was at that time engaged in the construction of the Bridgewater Canal. He had already completed the length connecting the Bridgewater Collieries at Worsley and Manchester and had successfully accornolished the then wonderful work of crossing the river Irwell at a considerable ele- vation above the level of its waters. A Loudon gentle- man who ins.de a special jeurney from the metropolis to see Brindiey's great eagineering work, thus describes I it under de of Septemb ir 30th, 1763. "Cis not long since I viewed tie arttti.sl ceriosities of L Indon. and j now have seen the natural wonders o( the Peak ; but none of them have giv,ri me so much pleasure as I now receive in suNeying the Duke ef Bri lee water's navigation in this country. His projector, the ineeatous Mr Brindled has indeed made such improvemeats in this way as are truly astonishing. At Barton Bridge he has erect.id a navigable canal in the air, for it is as high as the tops of trees. Wnilst I was surveying it with a mixture of wonder and delight four birges passed me in about the space of three minutes, two of. them being chained to- gether and dragged by two horses, who weut on the tetras of the canal, woereon, I must own, I durst hardly ' venture to walk, as la!most tremtled to behold the large , river Irwell underneath me, across which the navigation is carried by a bridge which contains upon it the canal of water with the barges in it, drawn by horses which walk upon the battlements of this extraordinary bridge.' Very likely Mr. Brindley encouraged by the success of his work conceived the idea of carrying his canal across the Mersey at Runcorn by a greatly enlarged elition of his Bartoa aqueduct, and on to Liverpool; plans of which he prepared and submitted in July, 1768 to a number of gent amen and merchants in Liverpool and Ruecorn. Want of financial support prevented its being carried out and the project was allowed to drop. In the year 1800, a somewhat c,lebrated London engineer, Mr. Ralph Dodd, submitted another scheme. 'Lois contemplated the erection of an arched bridge so constructed as to carry the canal and alse provide carriage and footways, but this ambitious proposal only shared the fate of it; predecessors. Mr. Dodd also made an alternative pan for the coustruction of a tunnel under the bed of the river, and a company was formed for its being carried out with Mr. Dodd as engineer, but difficulties arose amongst the promoters, and although a com- mencement is stated to have been made, the project was almost immediately abandoned. Thirteen years afterwards saw the birth of another proposal,and which so as not to interfere with the navigation of the Mersey boldly advocated a bridge of one span of 1,000 fee,-, brought down to the ground level on email side by sub- sidiary arches, and accompanied by what will be now considered the more singular proposal of paying for its cost of erection and maintenance out of the profits to be derived from the sale of the plentiful salmon, tante-

jug report, and which by his direction were published by his executor after his death. A publication which is a noble testimony to Telford's inventive genius, con• structive ability and unwearying devotion to his pro- fession. Though for reasons fully set out in his report, this design was not carried out, he, as it seems, regret- fully remarks "Though the project was aban- doned I recommended a bridge on similar principle over the Menia Straits, and this after much discussion, was adopted by Parliament." Although Runcorn was not honoured as the site of the first suspension bridge erected in England it can claim to have been the birth place of its design. On comparing Telford's plan for his Runcorn Bridge, anyone must be of opinion from the rnaoy points of semblance that with necessary adapta- tions to another site the Runcorn design was erected and is still spanning the Monis. Straits and connecting the mainland with the Isle of Anglesey. In 1817 Telford WAS again consulted by the committee and he undertook to revise his scheme but with the exception of substitut- ing wood for iron in some of its minor details, with the

, .. view of reducing its cost he presented again his first plan, and though its erection would have greatly added to his fame, lie was so impressed with the belief that its

probable limited utility as compared with the great road from the Midlacd3 to the North byway of Warrington,

would not justify so large an expenditure, that he would not advise the carrying out of his plan, although again and again appealed to. The next proposal was em- bodied in an Act of Parliament obtained in 1846 by the Grand Junction Railway Co., since merged in the Lon- don and North-Western Railway Co., but as the pro- posed bridge was only intended for railway traffic, and was never proceeded with further reference is unneces- sary. The propozal was, however revived by the Rail- way CQmpany in 1861 on improved lines. and in 1868 exactly a century after Brindley's in- terview with the Liverpool merchants with his plan of a Mersey Aqueduct the London and North Western Railway Company's combined railway and footbridge, was opened for public traffic. So recently as the year 1893. Sir Douglaa Fox prepared and sub- mitted a ple.n of a vehicular bridge which be proposed erecting about 50 feet to the east of the present struc- ture. In its general features it was intended to be a replica of it, but although strongly advocated, and brought under the notice of the Lancashire and Cheshire County Councils the project was allowed to drop. An elderly man named William Walter Arthur, who said he came from Warrington, appeared at Oldham on Wednesday, charred with wandering whilst of unsound mind.—Police Constable Potts said that on Tuesday night he observed the prisoner in Hudde,sfild-road, and in reply to a question he said that people were running after him to shoot him.—Prisoner : I have been suffering id-hellth,and I called at:the Town Hall, where the police laughed at me.—The Clerk : Where do you come from?— Prisoner : I have been lodging in Warrington ter the last five months. Memorial Window at Christ Church,

Latchford.

At the morning gletvice on Sunday at Christ Church, Latchfora, the new west window, dedilated to the memory of the late Mrs Malin Edeigen, of Quirnfield, Grappenhall, was unveiled. Toe window, which has been placed in the church by Mr. Malin Eielsten, repre- sents the scene in the House of Bethany,in which Mary Magdalene anoints the feet of our Lord. The deign has be3n chastely and artistically executed. The ser- vice was conducted by the Vicar,the Rev F A Screeton. The usual service of morning prayer was f Alowed to the end of the versiclea and responses, when special collects were recit3d. 'rho choir sang the anthem" Wnat are these ?" (Steiner), and after the singing of a hymn the clergy and choir proceeded t 3 the west end of the church, where the ceremony of 'unveiling the window took pace. Dediea.tory prayers were said,and the choir and clergy returned singing the " None Dimittis."—The sermon was preached ty the Vicar, who referred to the good works of the late Mrs Elelsten and said they re- j Aced in being privileged to jAn in paying this tribute of regard to one whose memory was still green and fragrant. They had made their offering, to God and His Church, and solemnly dedicated this tangible and lasting memorial to the revered and honoured dead on this anniversary, which lent to their ceremonial a singu- lar and pathetic propriety.

Warrington and the Indian Famine. A meeting of the Indian Famine Fund Committee was held on Tuesday evening,at the Town Hall,for the purpose of receiving the accounts and closing the fund. His Worship the Mayor presided.and re- ported that the total amount of subscriptions was £1,347 14s lid; bank interest,Xl 103 id ; payments. £5O 5.411 d; remitted to the Lancashire County Fund, £1,301 193 id, His Worship moved that the state- ment of the receipts and expenditure as submitted be approved and explained that the ac-ounts had been audited by Councillor Bennett.—Mr. Picks- vance seconded, and the motion was agreed to.— His Worship explained that, in spite of the de- mands made upon Warrington in other directions, the receipts this year for the fund exceeded the subscriptions in the year 1897, when there were no local calls of anything like the magnitude of those which had drisen during the past twelve months.— The amount contributed to the fund in 1897 Was £1,204 18s and £155 were paid direct It spoke volumes for Warrington that the feeling of in- terest in this particular matter had been so admir- ably shown by the townspeople. He believed they were really the only town in Lancashire where the subscriptions this year equalled the subscriptions in the year he had named. Tbat was eminently satis- factory. In conclusion, his Worship expressed hisl personal indebtedness to the Hon. Secretary, the Borough Treasurer, and especially to his valuable assistant, Mr Boyle, who, he said, had done the bulk of the work. He had rendered most valuable assis- tance, and had greatly facilitated the work of the committee. (Hear, hear, and applause.) A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Mayor for his _services on the proposition of the Rev. Father Whittle,seconded by Mr J W Hayward, J.P.In reply, his Worship said that what he had done had been a labour of love to him. His labour had been made exceedingly light by the valuable assistance he had had from the two gentlemen on his right and left.

The Late Mr. William Littler.

With deep regret we have to record the death after a lengthened illness, of Mr. William Littler, of 16, Leinster- gardens, Runcorn, who succumbed very early on Friday morning. The deceased gentleman was born on the 27th of September, 1841. He c mi. to Runcorn to prosecute lus trade in 1864, and while following bis employment at West Bank Dock, Widnes, sustained an accident which compelled him to relinquish his calling. On his recovery he accepted an appointment at the Runeorn Post Office, there being at that time only two postmen for the whole town, and in addition to deliver- ing letters they met the trains for mails and emptied the pillar boxes. Being possessed of indomitable energy and perseverance, he filled up his time by working on Runcorn Bridge, then in course of erection, and supply- ing milk and grocery to his numerous customers. He commenced as an insurance agent to the Prudential Assurance Company in 1859, maintaining his connection with that society up to the time of his death. He identified himself with the public life of the town, being for many years an esteemed member of the Runcorn Burial Board prior to their work being taken over by the Disttict Council. Shorcly after settling in Runcorn he assisted in the formation of a branch of the Grand United Order of 0 tifellows, known as the "Lily of the Valley," No. 575, which has th.cl an unbroken career of prosperity, and now numbers more members I than any lodge in the neighbourhood. •He filled various , offices in connection therewith, and held the position of treasurer at the time of his death. From boyhood to early man hood he was a singer in Norton village Church, but on removal to Runcorn he attended some special services being held at Old Brunswick, and afterwards became a member of the Wesleyan Methodist body. To- day there are many who will recall how zealously he entered into the work of open air missions, sick visiting, and other kindred efforts, thereby exerting a far-reach-I ing influence in raising the moral tone of the people. His sterling and consistent character won for litm the respect and confidence of all classes, while his genial disposition made him a favourite with old and young alike. He was a good husband and father, the interest and well-being of the home and family ever occupying the first place in his thoughts. Few men, if any, were better known in Runcorn than was the deceased gentle- man, and although owing to his long illness he has been for some time practically retired from the activities of his earlier life, he was not forgotten, and many friends sought him out, enjoying the pleasure of a few minutes conversation in refreshing old memories. He was a staunch teetotaler, and ever most strongly advocated the principles of total abstinence, which he believed to be necessary for the well-being and gener,l uplifting of the people. In politics he was an ardent Liberal, ever to the forefront in the interests of his party. As an in- stance of this, it may be mentioned that on the occa- sion of the recent general election he remained in bed the day previous to reserve strength which would enable him to go to the poll and record his vote. His was a type of character of which there are sadly too few and whose loss will be severely felt by his sorrowing family

A Blessing to Huramity.—What can I do to remove this cmetant, irritating feeling and lack of energy and brilliancy? It is a question often asked, and there seems no reply at once so safe and simple as Electricity. The forms of Electricity which are of the greatest utility are the indaced or Faradic current and the "Magnetic cur- rent," as utilised in the "Aiignetaire," and are, therefore of most interest to ordinary people, who naturally ask : "Will this renew the energies of those who are in a low state of health ? Will it cure disease?" The twofold question can be confidently answered in the affirmative. Take first, the people who can hardly be said to be really ill, but who are worn and fagged. Preachers, Writers, men of business, politicians, all brain workers who live in a condition of excitement and strain, belong to this class. Now for these people, worried, fevered, restless, sleepless, the Magnetaire is simply one of the best sedatives and restoratives known. It will calm, soothe, vitalise, and produce that requisite sense of exhilaration which is the invariable accompaniment of perfect health. People whe get into this languid, de- jected condition do not need medicine; they require re- cuperation. Even, if they suffer from liver or kidney complaints, which induce great depression of spirits, the "Magnetsire" will do them far more good than pills or potions.

urger question about India and the Colonies, and the formation of a truly Imperial army for Imperial defence, can hardly be dealt with in any bill that can be immediately drawn. Some of the details cannot be considered at present. Yet for the defence of India we must improve upon rather than abolish the present system, whilst for South Africa we shall have to make an increase in our troops or materially interfere with other arrange- ments. The responsibilities of Empire cannot be met without an increase in the army, and the burden will grow instead of diminishing. We must make up our minds to this increase, and no addition to our markets will compensate us for the outlay for long years to come. Bad trade and industrial distress will cause us to regard many subjects with anxiety that do not trouble us now, and we may be sure of this, that Imperial respon- sibilities will be heavier not lighter as the years roll on.

TIIE American Presiden ial The American Election contest has resulted in the . re-election of President MCKINLEY, without there being any protests against a second term, as was the case when General GRANT oflered himself a second time. The Situation was so peculiar in the United States that the claim for continuity of policy and of adminis- tration was very strong. A great change in foreign affairs, in domestic policy, and in the currency Would have meant something like a revolution,and the whole of Europe would have felt the shock. BRYAN has no complaint to make as to the Proper circulation of his views. He has had tfvo occasions for presenting them, and ho has stumped the States with an energy seldom displayed in recent American contests, if at all. He had some bemocrat stalwarts behind him, and he did his best to increase the number of his adherents by currency heresies, appeals to working-men, and the claim he made to represent the true American non intervention policyin external affairs. The PRESIDENT in Office could not compete with him in oratory. /le has not the gift, and it would have been un- seemly of him to have engaged in speech-making Ott any considerable scale. His policy has been eXpressed in deeds, and they are more eloquent than words. He has tried to combine respect for old traditions with deference to new ideas, and Ott the whole he may claim to have succeeded. The United States occupy a better position to-day than they did when President CLEVELAND retired, and probably the industrial classes have not suffered. President MCKINLEY may be regarded RS a safe man, and as such he compares favourably With Mr BRYAN, who, though he had noble ideas, seemed at times flighty and fantastic. It was a good thing that he should present to the eyes of the American people some of the dangers Which beset them, and he has even won some 8111Vort in unexpected quarters by his social Programme. But he has lost ground else- Where, and notably in New York State, cliiefly in consequence of his bimetallic views. The fluctuations of opinion in various States cannot Yet be given, however, with any certainty and Without more local knowledge than British Observers can claim to possess. States in America are influenced by considerations that cannot always be stated in a clear way, and probably trade and losses and gains due to the war against Spain and the troubles in China have causod a good many variations. The majority against Mr. BRYAN is so large as to mean that he must disap- pear from the Presidential contest, and content himself with local politics. He has done all that any candidate caulel do with the mixed programme he stood upon, and the world will be content to know that the gold standard is not in danger. It will be to the interest of the R 3publican party, once more triumphant and gaining seats in Con- gress as well as securing the Presidency, to make a good use of the victory. We may hope that the Tariff may be relaxed so as to increase trade with this country, and that there will be increased friendliness and co-operation in international affairs.

Public Rectitude— MANCHESTER has come Manchester Corpora- well out of the trying or- tion and its Lord IVlayor-Elect deal to which it has this Week been subjected by the investigation of charges against the probity of its Lord Mayor-elect, Alder- man HIGGINBOTTOM, and the only matter for regret IS that the verdict of the Special Committee was so unfavourable to the gentleman so gravely im- plicated. It would have been far more satisfactory Wall concerned, and much more reassuring to the citizens of Manchester,had the charges proved un- founded. Some of them, it is true, collapsed under the microscopic examination of the appointed tri burial, but in regard to certain contracts for erane9, the action of Alderman Hroorw- BOTTOM was stigmatised as "altogether im- Proper." On this finding there was nothi left for the Alderman to do but resign, and this he at once did with as much dignity as was possible under the circumstances. It is to the credit of 3Ylanchester that her municipal body did not act hastily in so serious a matter, or with any false Chivalry for the impugned Alderman. A sensible, firin, judicious course has been taken, and a verdict Unhesitatingly given which leaves the Corporation of this great city purer, stronger, and higher in the estimation of men than ever before. Alder- lean HIGGINBOTTOM has sustained perhaps as great a fall as was possible in municipal life, for he was to have been elected Lord Mayor this week, and for this reason there are some who would extend to him their sympathy. He may, on Purely personal grounds, be entitled to it, but we deprecate any suggestion that he does not deserve the fate which has befallen him. As the "Man- chester Guardian" says in its admirable comments on the case, "public men who fall short of the standard of public rectitude must suffer if that standard is to be maintained." There must, we Should imagine, have been much searching of hearts in the corporate bodies of other towns than 4anchester as a consequence of the charges against Alderman Higginbottom. There are few Corporations where there is not some such sailing hear the line, and men who have any conscience at all, even though innocent of anything actually !mProper, must have felt that their combiaation of business and public service was not one affording unalloyed satisfaction and peace of mind. Alder- than HIGGINBOTTOM was Chairman of a Cem- tanY supplying articles of the kind required a committee of the Corporation, of which he was also Chairman, though in this Case we believe there was no substantial cause of complaint against either him or the Corn- 13_atiY• "But," says the "Manchester Guardian," "it _ws4.3 in the highest degree indiscreet, and if infer- ences for which there was no foundation were drawn we fail to see how anyone could be sur- Prised. Men in public life owe it to the public

as well as to themselves not to put themselves in false positions of this kind. They may be deeply determined not to use their public posts to ad- vance their private interests in any way ; they may carry their determination into effect in the most rigorous fashion; and yet they do incalculable injury. Men of less scrupulous honour may succeed them in the equivocal positions and use them for their own private advantage, either with deliberate intention from the outset or because they are not able to resist temptation when it comes. Injury is done, too, by the sapping of the. confidence of the public in its public men when they are seen occupying equivocal positions; and laxity, or some- thing worse, is spread among the paid servants of the public, who find ready reasons for getting illi- cit gains when they suspect those in authority over them of breaches of public rectitude. It is for these reasons that we think it is not possible to discourage too strongly such equivocal positions as that in which Mr. HIGGINBDTTOM found himself in his Simultaneous chairmanship of the British Electric Works Company and the Manchester Oorporation Electricity Committee It is the appearance of 'evil, and the opening of the door to future evil, only less than the evil itself, that must be avoid3d."

Some time ago Mr. Winston Churchill made the remark that, notwithstanding so many other duties, Lord Roberts was the best of the war cor- respondents. Fettered 0,3 he was by details—and the facts are often sadly against the war correg, pondent who desires to be picturesque—Lord Roberts has managed to write sentences that went I home to the hearts of the people. His latest let- ter, which is addressed to the Press, touches with a fine tact and diplomacy a matter about which he feels very strongly. This is that care shall be taken not to place temptations in the way of the returning soluiers on their welcome home. He is as jealous for their honour and good name in the old country as he is pro-id of their valour and dis- cipline while under his command. He begs very earnestly that the welcome home may not take the form of" treating "the men to stimulants in pub- lic houses or streets, and so lead to excesses, degrading those whom the nation delights to honour. Lord Roberts alluded to the little weakness of Tommy Atkins in this matter with a singular felicity of expression. He fears that the " innate politeness" of the men may make it difficult for them to refuse these tokens of welcome; and, put that way, Mr. Atkins will see at once that no slur is cast on his character. Indeed, the latter has a hardly secondary import- ance in the emphasis with which it contradicts the "malicious falsehoods" as to the conduct of the British soldiers in Souch Africa. "They bore themselves like heroes on the battlefield," says Lord Roberts, "and like gentlemen on all °col- sions." It is a splendid tribute, and it is easy to understand how anxious Lord Roberts is that this record should not be sullied by the indiscretion of friends at home,plying men with liquor which they consent to drink in their emotion on the welcome back. We hope his wise words will be specially taken to heart in Warrington. 1

Pepito, the Spanish child who played the piano so vivaciously before the doctors and psychologists at the Paris Exhibition, is unexplainable. All the theories put forward fail to account for his skill. A pretty child of three years and a half, in light blue petticoats, with ringlets hanging down each side of his face—so small that the music stool is too low for him, and a number of books and cushions have to be put on it after it has beeu screwed to the top—that is the picture of the per- former. The performance is given with all the airs of a grown-up instrumentalist, and the won- derful thing is that so far from being merely mimetic or desultory the playing has the quality of original composition. The child has never had a music lesson in the ordinary sense of the term, and when any attempt is made to correct his fingering, which is inaccurate, he resents the in- formation and threatens to leave off playing —so peevish is his temperament. Some psycho- logists are using the case as a strong proof of pre- vious existence—the child has the attitude and style of someone recalling what had been learnt long before; but, as Mr. Lees remarks in the "Humanitarian," it is unwise to built up a general theory on an exception. These instances of precocity are exceedingly rare, and the danger is that the child's mind may be overturned by the attention given him and the desire to make a show thing of him. A few years ago there was in Brunswick a boy who at the age of two was able to read fluently various charactas—Roman,Gethic manuscript; and at four years of age was a store- house of facts, and especially familiar with dates He is being cared for, with special regard to his. bodily health,and his development is being awaited in patience.

-- Electric lighting in Warrington will,' according to Councillor Monks, be an actual fact by Christ- mas. Now that a definite date has been men- tioned shf,Tkeepors and-others who have had their walls and ceilings "wired "—and disfigured—will feel a little easier in their minds. They can look forward to astonishing our little, local world with a blaze of electricity which will make the Christmas show unique. The well-washed pigs which orna- ment the shop-doors of the butchers' fraternity, will, perhaps, discard the old-time orang3,and grin at the customer with an electric light between their jaws. These are the days of enterprise. We notice, too,that a number of lofty standards have been erected in Horsemarket-street, and Winwick- street presumably for the new light. They entirely dwarf the gas-lamps, and if the arc-light is to be as high as the standards it may benefit the birds but will probably give the people a stiff neck. This by the way, however. Things are at last moving— electrically speaking—and we must be thankful.

By the resignation of Alderman Harrison, the Warrington Town Council loses a well-tried and valued member. Latterly, ill-health has prevented him from attending to his duties but in his more active days he did much useful public work. For nearly 36 years be has been a member of the Council and for many years looked upon as the father of the Council. His long services were fittingly recognised when by the unanimous vote of the Council the Freedom of the Borough was conferred upon him on the occasion of the Jubilee of the Corporation. From 1883 until 1886 he satisfactorily discharged the duties of Mayor and Chief Magistrate and signalised his first year of office by originating the Christmas dinner to the poor of the town. During a long career he has endeavoured to serve the town which gave him birth and it must be some comfort to him in the evening of his days to know that his labour has not been fruitless.

A Provincial Letter,

Several important citieA and towns of the pro- vinces have had to go a-begging, as it were, for someohe to accept the highest civic honour the res- pective municipal authorities can confer, and the only way out of the difficulty appears to be by giv- Lig a salary where there is none and raising the figure in certain instances were there is. Nor does there appear to be any general objection to such a course wherever mentioned by way of suggestion. But the fact remains that no one likes to take upon hitus-lf the initiative, and one need' not, pehaps,ask why?

London, now entering upon a new era of self- government, might with advantage come to the provinces for a little elementary instruction. In- deed a correspondent of one of the Metropolitan dailies has been advising the new London council- lors to take a run down to Birmingham, as the "best governed city," but there is no particular reason why they should stop short in the Midlands, or at Birmingham. They might, perhaps, improve upon such a course by proceeding ''farther north." But tuition, after all, seems mostly needed, as yet, at the fountain head, with the electors of London themselves. Lord Itosebery has done what he can for the latter by pointing out to them the crying need of municipal refOrm, but, judged by the in- difference manifested in the election of the new Borough Councils, it would seem almost hopeless to expect sound and efficient administration iiit has to depend upon the exertions of the average Cock- ney. To ourselves, in the provinces, where nearly all ratepayers regard the selection of the men who are to manage civic business as of prime impor- tance, it was startling this week to find that only forty per cant, Of thg new Voters in the Metropolis had goite t 9 the poll, •

' At the risk of being charged with reVetitiiin, OnT cannot help reverting to a subject which LOta Roberts has said that he has very much at heart, and upon which Lord Wolseley recently spoke very plainly. In his letter through the Central News Agency the former makes an appeal to his country- men and women, who are looking eagerly forward to the return of the army from South Africa; and to the opportunity of giving our brave soldiers the welcome they deserve,not to let the giving of stimu- lants take the form of such welcome, thus leading the men into excesses which degrade and lower them in the eyes of the world. As every one knows, many friends of the "gentleman in khaki" threw discrbtion to the winds when the latter were going away, in the freedom and liberality wi'h which they " stood treat " ; so that in some of our towns "Tommy Atkins," instead of getting away at the proper time, put in an ignominious appearance before the magistrates next morning. It is believed that now the men are returning from the front, the danger of such consequences will be oven greater than before, and it is;in the .interests of the public that they should guard against any such contin- gency. Otherwise " Tommy's" bast friend will prove to be no friend at all. Hence Lord Roberts begs earnestly that the British public will "refrain from tempting his gallant comrades," and that they will aid rather in upholding the splendid reputation they have won for the Imperial Army; adding, "The troops bore themselves like heroes on the battlefield, and like gentlemen on all other occa- sions."

School Boards in most parts of the provinces have been slow to avail themselves of the additional powers conferred by the Act of Yarliament which came into force some three months ago. But now that both Parliamentary and Municipal elections are over, notice has been given by members of some of the more "progressive "Boards to move in the matter at the earliest opportunity. Bye-laws under the original Act and the Act of 1880,as a rule, require attendance at school between the ages of five and thirteen years, or until H.M. Inspector has certifie4 that a child on passing the fifth standard might be exempted before attaining the age of thirteen years,while the maximum penalty has been a fine of five shillings, including costs, upon con- viction before the justices sow, however, the age limit may be increased to fourteen years, under the Act, and it is open for the Board to fix the standard limit, the sixth instead of the fifth appearing to have preference. The Act also authorises the School Boards to fix the maximum penalty for irregular attendance at twenty shillings, instead of five as hitherto. The raising of the age for exemption appears to find more favour among School Boards generally than might have been anticipated when the matter was under discussion in Parliament.

The movement which has been started, and hasjust received promise of support from the Prince of Wales, for the erection of a statue to the late Lord Russell ot Billowen, reminds one that there is a probability of :;he re-introduction of his Preven- tion of Corruption Bill during the next Se4Sicoll of Peril merit, Tile measure strikas directly at a prac- tica common ea.ough among a certain class even of legal praetitioners, which was dealt with at the recent meeting of the Incorporated Law Society. But the commissions offered in these cases differ widely from the illegitimate bribes which are so frequently heard of in certain cases of com- mercial business. The fault lies in the fact that the commissions are taken with- out the knowledge of the client, and that the solicitor is not likely to be pre-disposed in favour of those companies who offer such inducements. The whole difficulty would be overcome if solicitors would disclose the fact that such commissions are paid. It would in no way reflect upon their probity, but it would be a straightforward course to adopt. As the matter stands, it is simply known to be a common, and perhaps not an unjustifiable practice, for a solicitor to take commission from an insurance company on a client's policy without the know- ledge of the latter. But the Prevention of Corruption Bill is looked forward to for the sup- pression of real evils which are known to exist, and which it has hitherto been found very difficult to deal with.

The agitation against boys being allowed to smoke is spreading, and School Boards in all parts of the provinces are resolving to petition Parlia- ment on the subject. If youths are not to be allowed to purchase drink, urged a member of the Kingsbridge Board, neither should they be author- ised to buy cigarettes. A medical writer says that whatever difference of opinion there may be as to the advisableness of smoking for men, there is none as to its pernicious effects upon boys. It effects the action of the heart and reduces the capacity of the lungs. Young men who are being trained as athletes are not permitted to smoke by theietrainers because, as they say "It is bad for the wind." But the argument, after all, that will appeal most forc- ibly to the young folks, is that smoking will stunt their growth. It has been proved, and it will be well t 3 press hcme the fact, that youthful smokers are shorter and weigh less than their comrades who do not smoke. Cigarettes, of course are espeli- ally injurious, and this truth cannot be too often enforced.

-.•,---4, Local Harriers.

Runcorn Harriers paid a visit to Sankey on Saturday to fulfil their inter-club race. They made the journey with 22 runners, but still lacking the service of W Ray and R Hadfield, whilst Sankey were without T Devlin and George Eastwood. Trail was laid over 8i miles of exceedingly heavy country including some good jumps. The visitors were determined to make a hot and closely contested race of it with the Sankey men, who were, up to meeting Runcorn, undefeated for the last three years. It was certainly a great disappointment to the home team to Meet with such crushing defeat. From start to finish Runcorn made the pace too hot for the Sankey men, and after four miles Ruucorn had it all their own way, and ran , out easy winners by 68 points, getting home the first seven men with little opposition. Runco:n men are confident that tney will be able to again turn the tables on Sankey men in their return engagement at Runcorn —On Saturday afternoon a very interesting and exciting contest was witnessed between the Farnworth and Bolton clubs. The route chosen was from Victoria- road, via Appleton, Farnworth, Rainhill and home, over Pex Hill, Hale and Ditton. No lea than twenty-seven members turned out, six of whom had papers to lay the trail. As the scent was strong the running was very close, and the finish was an exciting one. J. biker, cross-country champion, was the first home, closely followed by Messrs. Dainty and Handley. After the contest was over a splendid repast was provided at the Com- mercial Hotel. Songs were rendered by Messrs. Percival, Taylor and Ashwood. Mr. Martin accom- panied with his accustomed ability.
