Under the presidency of lir. Thomas Leach. Church-street. a large gathering of Liberals met at the Liberal Club Room, Regent-street, on Tuesday night, when Mr Joseph Brown, president of the Local Teachers' Union, gave an admirable address upon the "Difficulties of the Educational Question." In the course of his remarks Mr Brown ably con- tended for the nationalisation of the Educational System, drawing its finances from one centre—the Imperial lEs:chequer, and illustrated where reform was needed in the preeent system. An interesting debate followed in which Messrs W Millington, W B Brimelow, J M Frith, J Lea and others took part. Mr Brown, however, met all corners, and sustained with unanswerable ability the arguments set forth in his address.—Votes of thanks concluded the meeting —Next week Mr Mond, of Northwicb, will lecture at the club, and all Liberals are invite,6 SAVAGE ASSAULT ON WIDNES POLICE.

At Widnes Police Court on Monday, Thomas Dearden was charged with being drunk and dis- orderly, and also with assaulting Sergeant Carter and P.C. Turner.—The prisoner pleaded guilty to thefirst charge and not guilty to the second.— Ser eant Carter stated that at 3 20 on Sunday after- noon he was in Viridne-road in company with. P.C. Turner. Prisoner was there together with other m en, shouting and creating a disturbance. He asked him several times to go home, but he refused. Asshe knew where prisoner lived he took hold of hi arm to lead him home when he gave witness a violent kick on the ankle. lie managed to get the handcuffs on him, but, after going quietly tor one hundred yards prisoner tripped him up, and While he was lying on the ground he kicked him viol6ntly on the left temple. He also bit him on the hand and took a piece of flash clean out.—P.C. Turner, and a M.r Brown of Shams Cross gave corrobarative evidence.—The Bench said that the assault on the police was a serious one, and it was cowatdly indeed to bite a man. Prisoner would be fined 2e 64 and costs for being drunk and disorderly, and would have to go to gaol for a month in each case of assaulting the police. A HEAVY FINE,

Andrew Byrne, an Irishman, and mate of the schooner "Pearl" of Connah's Quay, was on Tuesday brought up before Messrs F J Norman and W Timmins, and charged with indecent behaviour in a public house, the "Brunswick Inn," Brunswick- street. Whilst in the dock prisoner was seized with an apopletic fit and fell and injured his scalp, which necessitated the case being remanded to the following day.—On Wednesday the magistrates were Messrs F J Norman and W Handley.—Byrne pleaded not guilty to the charges, as he had been drinking all week and did not know exactly what happened. The cases were proved by the state- ments of Martha Ellen Watson. barmaid, and Mary Ann Sleeman, landlady of the Brunswick Inn. Prisoner protested his innocence, and said that the barmaid Watson was drinking with the sailors and playing dominoes with them in the house, where he had been night after night drinking. P.C. Wil- son gave evidence of the apprehension of the prisoner, whom he found in High-street, drunk. de received 'information of the case from the land- lord of the house. The Bench decided to convict and fined Byrne 40s and 17s 64 costs in the first case, and Ns and 7s costs in the second, or in de- fault 21 days. Prisoner: Well sir, Ido not think I am gnilty. It is very hard sir, indeed very hard.

FRODSHAM.

PanocniiL CODIMITTRE.—The monthly meeting of the Frodsham Parish Council, acting 45 a Parochial Com- mittee, met on Friday evening. A letter was read from the Manchester Ship Canal Company with regard to the alleged holding up of the water in the River Weaver from which it was gathered that it was almost impossible to maintain the water at a uniform level at any point by the company.—Mr. Illidge stated that the water in the river Weaver bad been held up nearly sixteen inches above the normal height for the greater part of the day.—On the proposi- tion of Mr. Shore, seconded by Mr. Williams it was re- solved that the committee cannot accept Mr. Hunter's explanation.—The Surveyor reported as to the sinking of the main road near to Mr. G Weave's, which he attri- buted ta the quicksands entering into the sewerage pipes, when it was decided that the sewerage pipes be cleansed as far as possible from thegnanhole and that the Surveyor have the matter underlis observation.

THE FRODSHAM LORDSHIP WATER SUPPLY.—The rate- payers of the Frodsham Lordship are to be congratulated on the satisfactory completion of their water supply, which has been under consideration for the last ten years. In 1891 the township acquired an abundant supply of pure water, but the Lordship was unable to come to terms for its supply, on account of the higher elevatidn of the principal residential property, and the question was shelved until last year, when the private water supplies, drawn from wells in the district, were found to be both inadequate and unsatisfactory on analysis; then in the interests of both town- ships, arrangements were made to extend the scheme, and plans prepared and submitted to the Local Government Bard, who seat down their inspector, Mr. G. A. Sandford Fawcett, A.M tn hold •an inquiry, aud he report-NI favourably on the scheme, and sanction was obtained from the Local Governm-• Board, for the sum of £3,400, to carry it out. RUNCORN NOT ES

[By a Correspondent].

My first duty this week is to remonstrate with the Runcorn magistrates who last Wednesday pre- sided over the local court. Mb case was that of a sailor Byrne,who pleaded not guilty to a charge of indecent exposure. There were two cases against the man, and each was supported by but one wit- ness. One offence was alleged to have been com- mitted on the Saturday and thom other on the follow- ing Monday. There was no corroboration whatever. True the offence was a despicable one, but in this freedom loving England of ours,surely a man should not be so harshly dealt with as to have to pay 40s and costs in each case for an offence, he denies., and which is only attested by another witness. Tho man said he was drunk, this was clrroborated ' by P.C. Wilson. The other witnesses testified to tne man's sobriety, and here will be seen toe crux of the case. if toe man was sober, and sober too on the evidence of the single witnesses, who by the way convicted him in each separate case, surely his word should have carried as much weight as theirs did. Any wav,it is apparent that Byrne, who is a middle-Aged man, has always conducted him- self with decency whilst sober, and if he was not "drunk" on the evidence whica convicte I him ought to have 1:1,,en entitled to credit for what he said.

Let me continue, for the sailors really have my sympathy Byrne's remarks about "girls and domi- noes," taken generally have more in them than meet the eye at first glanc?, They open up to us a subject upon which it would be well for the Tem- perance Party, which by the way is far too lethar- gic for the task they have at heart, to Tend a little energy investigating. Thera is a good deal of it carried on in Runcorn —(sub-zosa of course)—for the sailors benefit; and to it many a sea-faring man's wreck is traceable. Where the difficulty lies, everyone knows, is the accumulation of sufficient proof; bu;, a determined body of Temperance workers are surely, not going to be deterred from engaging in so goad a cause by so small a difficulty. As for the police, I have nothing but commendation to bestow upon them, for I know from personal experience the " bobby with a heart': has beets a better friend to society than society has ever been to him.

What is Ruicorn's po3ition with regard to the Proposed Transporter Bridge? Tais is a question upon the tip of everyone's tongue, and for the sake of the curious I will now state it. The first pro- posal of the Urban District Council of Runcorn was for the investment of £5.000 in the concern, subject to the Sanction of the 65cal Government Baard,and subject also to the Council having power to appoint a director. The promoters reply to that letter was they could not sea their way, to accept the £5,000 Plitt] the condition attached. Still they were vary pleased to see that the Urban District Council had offered to take up .C 5,000 wont] of shares in the Bridge.

The next step taken by the Council was to appoint a deputation to go further into the matter, and the result was they found there was very little hope of getting a directorship for the £5,000; and, by the way, this is apparent to all for the full capital is £lOO,OOO and the full directorship nine. A simple division will easily show that at the least a directorship should not carry with it less than £lO,OOO. The deputation reported to the adjourned meeting. Here the exceptional reasons for Runcorn being differently treated to other places—such as the relinquishing of the ferry, and its low ratable value etc.,—were set forward, and it was decided to hold to the original resolution, and place the facts before the pram3ters.The promoters' reply was to the effect that the Council's letter was duly con- sidered by them, and nicer carafully going into the matter they regretted they could not alter their decision already communicated to the Council.

Subsequent to that the promoters wrote to the effect that it was proposed to subscribe the capital of the company at an early date, and if the Council after further consideration desired " to subscribe for £lO,OOO in shares, and have the right of nominating one director," they would be glad to hear from them before Tuesday, the 13h inst, on which date there would be another meeting of the directors. Here it will be seen is the first offer of a directorship, and as there is nothing in the Act to force the promoters into giving the Council a directorship, it is a very generous offer. What is the Council's reply going to be?

On Thursday morning several Emembers of the Council acting in Committee, expressed the opinion that they did not feel justided in moving from £5,000 to £lO,OOO without in some way having an expression from the general body of ratepayers, and it was finally arranged to hold a meeting of ratepayers on isloaday next, and that the voice of that meeting shall decide the question.

On the Tuesday morning the Council will re- assemble in committee, and give effect to . the ex- pression of opinion at the ratepayers' meeting, and forward a reply to the promoters in time for their meeting the same day. Finally, let me advise that the bridge scheme has all the elements of a scund concern. From the beginning, the gentle- men interested,while fully conscious that the bridge will return an ample dividend, have felt, and have expressed the opinion that the local authority on either side of the Mersey should have a voice in its management. The promoters are men who have served the public in many capacities for the past quarter of a century ; they have inspired and carried out large public undertakings in the towns with which they have been identified; and the public spirit with which they have been animated admits of no denial. Wid- nes has recognised this and has demonstrated its confidence in the solidity of the Company, and the probable future financial success of the Bridge, by subscribing no less than £25,000. Widnes is thus entitled to two directors,and will certainly get them Is Runcorn going to be left lagging behind? llt has ever been a common complaint, that ' the Commissioners in the days when the London and North-Western Railway Bridge was being promoted, displayed a lament- able lack of enterprise, and a WOefqi l'f4ttt of knowledge of what the town, did require and might require in the course of a few years. If the ratepayers are wise they will have a "stake," and a good " etalce,"in the Tnnekorter Bridge, which as the years roll on must blia4M6 tile chief means of communication bet Ween Runcorn and Widnes, Runcorn, I belieVe, has seen its worst days of con,:, ' mercial depression, and should no new indus- try settle here for some years to come, there is bound to be big developments in the shipping trade. If Runcorn people invest .£lO,OOO, they are only investing, compared with Widnes, in ratio to the rateable value of the two towns; and it would be an everlasting disgrace to the ratepayers of Runcorn should they prove to be less public spirited and less enterprising in this matter, as they have certainly proved themselves to be in the past, in regard to certain undertakings which are now not only the pride but the boast of Widaesians.

HELSBY SEWERAGE AND WATER. At a meeting of this Committee, Mr James Taylor pre- siding, the committee appointed to consider as to the acquisition of a site for sewerage purification works reported in favour of such a course and recommended the purchase of a site belonging to Lord Haddington for whom Mr Robert Dodd, ot Tarporley is agent and that the necessary agreement be entere:l into with ‘t r Dodd. The clerk read a letter from Messrs H Bancroft and Son, of Manchester declaring it irregular and unfair to com- pletely condemn a sewerage scheme upon the report of a local surveyor. The question of the purchase of the Helsbv and District Waterworks came up for considera- tion whereupon Mr Taylor being a director of that con- cern retired from the chair and Mr James Wnite was appointed chairman in his stead. It was then resolved that the subject be deferred and the clerk ascertain from Mr Radford the number of gallons of water that would be required for flushing thn sewers provided for in his scheme.—At a special meeting of the committee on the 27th October an agreement for the purchase of the land for sewerage purification works was submitted and the committee recommended the District Council to affix their seal to the document.

Tne minutes were passsd on the motion taf Mr Linaker. THE rEIEI, WALL NUISANCE. AN ISLE OF MAN PHYSICIAN COMPLAINS. MR. NAYLOR RETURNS TO THE ATTACK.

Mr Hebert Marshall, physician and surgeon, Bedford HOUS3, Dsuglas, Isle of Man, wrote under date November Ist to Mr Ashton, clerk to the Rural District Council:— Dear Sir : Whilst on a visit to Mr Naylor, of Cuerden Hall, Thelwall, about a fortnight ago, I got a dose of the abominable stench arising out of the brook from the adjoining tannery which brought on a most violent and sudden illness. On several occasions previously 1 have had cases under myobservation brought on by the same cause. This is not only a great nuisance, but it is posi- tively dangerous to life, and not only is th e stream po:- luted, but the air also, and it must surely be caused by a differeut method of tanning than that usually adopted the smell arising from which is not nearly so offensive nor dangerous to health. May I respectfully point out to your Council, as Medical Officer of Health of a fairly large area in the Isle of Man, that even as a nuisance it would not be tolerated here, to say nothing of its in- jurious effects to health. Writing to Mr George F Ashton, from Da Vers Hotel, Kensington Palace, London. W., under date November 3rd, Mr R A Naylor said: According to the newspaper report of your last meeting Mr Linaker is reported to hive said he did not understand the question of the Tannery Nuisance which has been so long before your Council, nor did he think anyone else did: I presume this about represenl's the position of your Council. On August 24th I sent you a letter giving you six distinct cases of sickness, directly due to the tannery smell. Two of these cases can be certified by medical men. In my last I gave your Council particulars of a case where a Medical Officer of Health almost lost his wife through nhaling tin poison. and I could furnish you with a long ist of victims. 1 sgr, Monday two ladies were made 111 by the horrible smell, and one of them is not yet re- covered. I bear the nuisance is not in any way abated and the daily reports indicate thatjthe filtration process will not abate it.

Admiral Jones-Parry, in accordance with notice, moved tt at"this Council clean out the cesspit and brook adjoining, Waste-lann in the parish of Grappenhall, and into which the refuse from Messrs Long's Tannery is turned, and that the expense thereof be charged against the owners of tb3 adjoining land." He said there waa abundant evidence that there was a nuisance. The sub- ject bad been before the Council for twelve months and mov:d this resolu:ion in the hope that it would bo got rid of. Tao Clerk amid the Claud' ought to call upon the 0 wears of the property to clean out the ditch if it were a nuisance. If the Council did the work themselves they had no power to recover the cost. Inspector Farrington said Messrs Lang, if they were asked, would clean out the ditch and cesspool. _ _ _

Mr Woodward moved and Mr Linaker seconded a resolution, requesting Messrs Lang to do so, and this was carried,

ADMIRAL V. DOCTOR.

Admiral Jones-Parry now referred to a denial by Dr Adams that he had declared the effluent from Messrs Long's Tannery at Grappenhall a nuisance dangerous to health, and he quoted numerous extracts from the newt- papa's to show that the doctor had dune so. He admit- ted, howaver, that he could not find that the doctor had done so specifically, but he (Adaairal Parry) had said in the doctor's presence in the Board Room that the doctor bad so declared it, and silence, he said, gave omsent. Dr. Adams repeated his declaration that he had never said the (ffluent from the Tannery was a nuisance injurious to health, 'and added that he never would do. Re had only dealt with that matter in regard to the pollution of streams under the Rivers Pollution Preven- tion Act. Be admitted, however, that this effluent corn. bined with the contribution from the dwellings on the banks of the stream constitute I a nuisance dangerous to health.

There was no other business of public interest.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL. The Combination. Newton-le-Willows v. Bangor. Played at Newton on Saturday, before a moderate attendance. rh3 visitors brought a strong team, and Newton were also fully represented. Newton kicked off. and the ball was immediately taken into the visitors'. half. The custodian was not,however, seriously troubled and the Bangor for wards were soon operating in the home goal. A. nice piece of work by Tilorris3ey enabled Kenny to gat going on the home right, and a correct centre from the foot of this player was met by Hill, who compaled the visitors' dustodian to come out of his goal to save. Bangor obr.ained the first point from a breakaway 15 minutes after the start. This success spurred the visitors on to further efforts, and five miaute3 later a centre from the left ; wing was met by the right wing outside, and Bangor I were two in front. Newton seemed to liven up after this and Worsl.!..y raced away with the ball, and when close in crossed over to the right. From there the ball was dropped right under the bar, and Lee bad no diffi- culty in reducing the visitors' lead. Just blfore the interval the visitors increased their score to three from a corner. Half-time; Bangor 3, Newton 1. On changing ends the combination of the Newton team somewhat , improved and the front line were able to trouble the Bangor defence. They increased their score by 2 goals, but Bangor again got the lead, and at the call of time, the score stood, Bangor 4 goals, Newton 3.

English Cup, garlestown v. White Star Wanderers. Earlestown safely negotiated another round of the qualifyiug stages of the above cup on Saturday, when they gave White Star Wanderers their quietus on the Mesnes ground. Earlestown have been singularly fortu- nate in their ties in the cup competition so far as all three rounds have been played at home,notwithstanding, the fact that they were drawn away on two occasion. In the first instance they were down to visit Swinton Town but the Yorkshire club decided to change the venue, and in the second, the White Scar ground was not the reqaired dimensioas and the match had therefore to be played It Earlestewn. However, they had no diffieutty in dis- posing of White Star, although the score ot two to one would lead anyone who had not seen the match to think that the game had been closely contested. Earlestown always had their opzeonents well in hand and had they infused a little more spirit into their play, they could have made the margin far larger than it was. The visitors brought a strong eleven and Earlestown were represented by the same team that were defeated by Crewe Alexandra the week previous, except that Linton took Lee's place at centre forward, the latter player not being eligible. Earlestown won the toss and Roberts started on behalf of the Star with the hillin their favour. The opening exchanges were mostly colitested in the home half the visitors' forwards playing with a dash that quite baffled the home defence with the result that Cottam in trying to clear a shot from Ireieg, put the leather through his own goal, fifteen minutes from the start' This early reverse livened Earlestown up and the ball was quickly carried in the vicinity of Hodge. A pretty csntro from the Lett was met by Bartley and baneed with terrific ferce agaieat tue uprignt, the Dail being cleared from the rebound. A moment later Settle got in another correct centre and this was met by Whitehouse and promptly placed into the net. rem encouraged the horsesters again got going and splendid work by Leach was loudly applauded. A few minutes before the adjourn- ment Settle dribbled close in and then passed to White- house who slipped over the ball but quickly recovered himself and banged it past Hedge for the second time, and the inter eat came with Earlestown leading by two to one. The second half was fiercely contested but although Earlestown did practically the whole of the pressing they could not add to their :Core, and the fine' therefore arrived with the half•time score unaltered„

ACCIDENT AT VULCAN FOUNDRY.

On Saturday morning Jona Evison, living in Heald-street, Earlestown, was engaged working under the jib of a crane at V alcaa Foundry, when from some cause or other the jib fell. Fortunately for Evison he was working at, a crank, and to this fact he undoubtedly owes his life, a- the crank re- ceived the weight of the jib wen it fell. Evison escaped with a severe shaking and a sprained ankle.

ODDFELLOWi ANNIVERSARY AT EARLESTOWN.

The Annual dinner of the Loyal Viaduct lodge of Oddfellows, M.U., was held at the Wellington Hotel on Saturday evening. About 60 members sat down to a repast served in Host Thorpe's best style, and they did ample justice to the good things provided. After the cloth had been removed the usual social gathering took place, Bro G Wilson, P.P.G.M.,presiding, and Br° J Jones,P.G.,occupying the vice chair. The chairman in opening the pro- ceedings said they were pleased to have the presence of Bro Tunstall, who had brought with

him an old friend, Bro Burgess of West Derby diss- trict. He proceeded to refer to the deficiency ques- tion, which he said was a vital one affecting the 31.41. He did not agree with the method the Direc- tors of the M.U. had in clealing with the question, because there were many lodges which were able to pay away surpluse,, through not having an excess in sickness, whilst there were others which could not pay anything away, and who had to labour under difficulties. Their lodge was such an one, they had excessive sickness, and they had suffered in this way for the past few yearsi , and he did not think there was another lodge 4 in the whole of the M.U. which had suffered 'more than they had in funeral levies. As a lodge they had lost £BOO, since the formation, in payments to the district, It was a dead loss and to those who formed the Management Com- mittee it was a great question, and they felt sore - upon it. He was of opinion that a second or third levy would put them straight in about five years. He concluded by proposing the toast "The Queen. land royal family," which was drunk with musical honours. The toast "The Manchester Unity and Board of Directors," was given by Bro Burgess and responded to by Bro J Tunstail in a lengthy speech in which he gave numerous statistics in regard to the Order. "The Viaduct Lodge" was proposed by Dr Pattersou, and responded to by Bro J Thane,. secretary, who submitted his annual financial state- ment, compiled for the 25 years ending 1899. The capital of the Lodge in 1875, was £922 19s 3cl. In contributions since then they had received £6,568 4340. In funeral donations (report by the district) they had paid £B9O. The interest was £1,893 4s Bd, and 'sundry receipts amounted to .£l4O 17s Thad. To_ the sick they had paid out £5,198 Os Bd, and in funeral donations to members (wives and widows), £B9O. In funeral levies to the district £1,490 19s 50 had been paid out, and sundries accounted for £9B, leaving a capital halaece of £2,737 193 10d. Other toasts were proposed and duly honoured, and numerous songs were given by Bros Wright. Mawdsley, J Appleton, J C Seddon, J Clare, C Pear- son, J T Banton, J Hunt, W Phillips, and J Allan„,

WAS HE FOLLOWED?

There was nobody following William Perring, yet his fear, or feeling, that his movements were being tracked made him shake like a dry leaf on a tree. And so it would anybody. Nothing is more scaring than the thought of danger from secret enemies. Why,men have gone mad over the mere dread of them. But Perriug had no reason to suppose he was followed; in truth he kiteto it couldn't be ; his memory and his conscience assured Win of that.

'Here's the tit( ; you can see how matters were for yourself. Perrin is a d/ck labourer he lives at 45, Duke-street, Devcnport, and is thirty-nine years old. That he is naturally a strong and healthy man goes without argument, other wise he wouldn't be a dock labourer; it isn't a ctiling for weak chaps. Well, up to April, 1899, he was up to his work sound in wind and limb. Then he began to feel mean and stale. He says he had a nasty tightness and heaviness about his chest, and his victuals s)ttled down inside of him like a lump of lead in soft ground ; and he took to having spells of heart palpitation that shook him as the engine shakes a ship when the screw kicks out of water. He managed, did poor Perring, to pull through his day's work and then got home and dawn on the sofa as quick as he could. His supper was ready, but he had no use for it, he couldn't eat,

In telling all this one day—the 30th of November, 1899, it was—Peering said" My body ached all over and I got so nervous and irritable I couldn't bear to have my own children about me. I was that touchy and miserable, I merely wanted to be let alone. "Gradually I got so bad that when out on the dock the idea would come into my head that I was folic ?red, and I d shake like a leaf. "The doctor said I was suffering from indigestion, and that my nervousness and weakness all came from that ; but he wasn't able to help me. "Then one bf my mates said, Why don't you try Mother Seigel's Syrup ? It cured me of the same thing. "So I got a small bottle and it did me some good, and I thought if I kept on using it I might be a well man yet. Any way I resolved to give it a chance. "As I continued taking Mother Seigel's ,:yrup— and a good mother 'to lots of psor sufferers she is—l felt better and better. The tightness and weight at my chest went away and I at my food and enjoyed it as I vied to do. " In all I took Mother S.3ig-l'i Syrup several weeks and then I stepped; and now I am as well as ever I was in my life." _

Perring (good luck be wits him!) is ebwn on the d ock regularly on work days, and ne only thing that "follows" him is the regret that his mite didn't shout out, "Mother Seigel's Syrup!" in the mxith of April, 1899.

The Path to Destruction.

Commenting on the forthcoming visit to War- rington of the Rev John Gould, who has been appointed by the National Council of Evangelic al Free Churches to conduct a mission in the town, ' " The Warrington Circuit Wesleyan Methodist Church Record" eays :—The general idea of the mission is, to bring all the churches into line in one great effort to win men for Christ, by bringing the Gospel invitation within the reach of everybody, high and low rich or poor, not only in the towns and cities of our land but also in the villages and hamlets. It is a noble conception and without doubt was inspire iof God. The task truly is great but the need of this evangelistic work is urgent and increases. The growth of the Church has not kept pace with the growth of the population; nor hast the fervour and zeal of Christians kept pace while the activity of the forces of evil. One has only to think of our own town, with its.many places of worshipoill too sparsely attended on the Sabbath, and almost entirely neglected during the week, while the streets are thronging with of giddy, thoughtless, and Godless youto, wnos im- patient of restraint, have cast from them all ease of responsibility, and, in too many instances, of even shame and self-respect, and are carelessly and aimlessly drifting along the downward path, the and of which is destruction. It is sad to think how many of these may have passed through our Sab- bath schools, and been the subjects at some time or another of religious influences.

Though defeated, Stock- pott were not at all disgraced, for on form Runcorn will ,tAke a deal of beating, for with a fast set of forwards and clever three-quarters, to say nothing of the, kicking powers of the Cheshire back, they ate a first-class combination. ONLY A BEAN.

A Miner's Story.

To all concerned in smelting or mining, the recant experience of an Ardsley man will bi read ju3t now with interest. Ardsiey. near Wakefild, i 3 c entre of iron and coal mining% and Mr Samuel Bedford is one of the bast kaown mu in the district To an interviewer the other day he said, "I am, as you may see, a collier by trade, bat, unfortunatily, my work has not heen welcome to me for nearly ten years, as during most of that time I hava suffered almost cintinually with an affection of the kidneys. I:',ople who have suffered in this way will know what that mans. At times the pjas in my -back were so bad that I was bent nearly double, nod it was only with the greatest difficulty and agony that I could straLzht-Fli myself up. So great were my sufferings that I was totally unfit to follow my employmeat, and, for a whole 18 months, I could not go down the pt or do a stroke or work. Added to this I I suffered terribly from indigestion, and for six months I was unable to eat flesh meat without suffering for hours afterwards."

"But cou'd the doctors do you no good ?" queried the visitor. " Not a bit." 2aid Mr. todiord. " I tried several doctors, including a Leeds physician. They gave me lota of medicine, but Li was all no i-zootl. They all agreed that mine was a bad ease, and that did not make f ael more cheerful, I esti tell you " " I suppose you have also tried Patent Medicines ?" " I had been trying Patent Medicines tor a low, time, of ' all sons, in fact I must have spent moulds in them . Some of them, it is true, seemed to do me good for a time, but I soon fell back into my old way and gave them up as a bad j)b, and thought the doctors were right when they said mine was a bad case." " Well, I can% say you look a bad min now," said the interviewer. " Never felt better in my life," answered Mr. Belford. " I said I had lost faith in Patent Medicines, but there is one medicine I shall always stick to. I came to hear of it through an ad- vertisement in "The Yorkshire Post," giving an account of a cure in a case similar to mine (which seemed to me incred;ble), by taking Charles Forth's Bile Beans for Biliousneae. The pains in my back had disappeared, and I found that I was once more really reslored to health. To-day my appetite is good. I can eat a hearty meal, and what is just as important to a man who has his Living to make, I can do a ao9-1 day's work. I am now working repo- larly every day, using pick and shovel." As Mr. Bedford bade his parting gue-t good-be, he had another word of gratitude for Bile Beans—remark- ing that he had great pleasure in making the above statement,and would always recommend Bile Beans to anyone suffering as he had done. FABIAN LECTURE IN THE DRUIDS' HALL.

What to do with thej Liquor Traffic.

The first of a series of F Lbian educational lectures was delivered by Mr S D Shallard in the Druidb' Hall, Sankey-street, last Thursday evening. The subject was" What to do with the liquor traffic." The Rev FE Powell, MA. (vicar of St James') pre- si

The CHAIRMAN said he had two very urgent rete,ons tor being present at that meeting. Firstly from a perusal of Fabian literature during the last few years he had found hipself being drawn more and wore to the Fabian position. It was very seri- ous for him and for others who felt themselves drifting in the same direction if the indictment of Mr Geoffry Langtott in this month'a "Fortnightly [ Review " could be sustained, According to this [ writer anyone who had aNything to do with social-' ism must either be a fool or a knave. The writer [ denounced both Liberals and Conservatives alike for reading the opinions of such mischievous, monsters as those who advocated anything in the. direction of socialism. He (the Chairman), was pleased to be that. night the leader of such a pack. (flear, hear.) Ile only diff,tred from Mr Langtottiu believing that the paek so far from degrading and destroying England was rather engaged in re - morsele,,sly pursuing those causes which were at work destioying and degrading much that was best in our national life. It was true that individualism was a force which they must all respect. (Rear, hear.) It was a force which had accomplished much in the past, much that was good and much also that was bad. Social- ism was a force with which the world was called upon to reckon, and he belleved that both the forces he had mentioned were necessary in human society because it was in the opposition of forces that true progress was made. Tnat night Mr Shaliard w..s present to tell them how to deal with the liquor trsffic. As a clergyman, with daily, almost hourly experience of the terrible effects of our unhappy drinking cus- toms, the persoual and moral aspect of the temper- ance question touched him very forcibly indeed. Those who read tne newspapers must have been sickened at the account of those drunken orgies which disgraced London on Monday night. (Hear, hear.) He thought such terrible scenes at the dawn of the 20th century should make them consider whether whatever reputation for self-control and self respect we as a nation might have, was not in grave danger of being altogether forfeited. (Hear, hear.) In addition to the moral and personal aspect of the temperance question, there was the politica and social aspect, and the solution of the problem lay in the municipalisation of the liquor traffic. (Hear, hear.) Mr. SHALLARD pointed out that it was necessary to strike out a new path in temperance reform. In spite of all the efforts of temperance workers], very little progress had been made, in fact the tnirst for blood during the last quarter of a century seemed to have produced a greater national thirst for drink. (Hear, hi-ar.) It was necessary to take some other step. The lecturer proceeded to deal in detail with the three serious proposals before the country for dealing with the drink traffic namely local veto, the Gothenburg system, which had been I advocated by the Bishop of Chester, and the munici- palisation of the traffi e With regard to the first proposal, the lecturer said it would lead to prohibition in certain neighbourhoods. He con- tended that the first aim of temperance advocates ought to be directed towards preventing drunken- ness rather than the stoppage of the sale of drink. He showed by numerous illustrations in America land elsewhere that prohibition had failed to bring about the desired object. Sheebeening and a con- tempt for the law followed the introduction of [ prohibition and it was generally the case that just ! outside the prohibition area a large number of [ drinking saloons were to be found which catered in various ways for those inside the prohibition area. In dealing with this matter they must take human ! nature as they found it. He referred at length to [ the Gothenburg system and pointed out that this bad only been a partial success owing to the fact that the system only dealt with spirits and not with wine and beer. So far as the system had been [ adopted it had certainly reduced drunkenness from !spirit drinking. The profits had been utilised on objects which tended to be counter-attractions to the trade and the managers of the drink shops were only given a commission on non-ilcoholic liquors. His thought they could learn a lesson from the ! Gothenburg system and that was the desirability of municipalising the drink traffic in this country. By getting the control of the trade, the public would be able to bring about a great improve- ment and they would be able to do this ! without receiving opposition from persons with private interests at stake. In communities where there was a strong feeling for prohibition this could he brought about much easier when the trade was in the hands of the public than at the present time when it was to the interest of private individuals to sell drink.

RESIGNATION OF ALDERMAN JOSEPH HARRISON.

Owing to continued ill-health Alderman .Joseph Harrison has resigned the position of High Bailiff of the Warrington County Court a position which he has held for the last 30 years and upwards. Mr. Harrison has been connected with the court ever since it was instituted and while he has been High Bailiff there have been three judges, viz., Judges Harding, Wynne Ffoulkes, and biwynne James and three Registrars, I%lessrs William Nicholson, E J Nicholson, and T J Ridgway. Under the County Courts Acts the position of High Bailiff now dc- volves on the Registrar, Mr. T. J. Ridgway. In addition to his resignation of the position of High Bailiff of the Warrington County Court owing to continued ill health, Alderman Harrison has also for the same reason tendered his resignation of the position of alderman of the County Borough of Warrington.

Alderman Ilarrison entered the Council as repre- sentative of the South West Ward in November, 1864 and has thus merved thirty-six years without a break. On the 3rd of April, 1897, by the unani- mous von of the Council, the honorary freedom of the Borough was conferred upo'n him in recognition of his emin-mt public services. lie was the oldest member of the Council.

Alderman Harrison's resignation causes an Alder- manic vacancy, In accordance with the decision of the Council to confer the honour upon the Councillor who has given the longest continuous service, the po•ition will fall to Councillor F W Monks. In that event there will be a vacancy in Whitecross Ward.

Runcorn F.C. Committee and Free

Tickets.

Sir,—l as a member of the Runcorn Football Club almost since its commencement would like to ask a few ,questions. There are complimentary tickets sent to the secretary to be given to the committee, and as it is a fact that all of them cannot get away,what is done with the tickets that are not used ? Members of the commit- tee used to have th. ir names printed on tfloir cards showing they were on the committee, and when they went to a strange ground they had only to present their cards and were allowed on. Now I know that it is im- possible for all the coramittee to attend these away matches. What becomes of these extra tickets? Does the secretary return them or does he distribute them amongst his personal friends, who in some cases are not members of the club and simply follow the team when they want a cheap outing. My enthusiasm for the game, although only a workingman, has led me at various times to follow the team to all the away matches. This, as everyone knows is a great expense, and I have never once had a tick-t given to me, although I have seen them given to non-committee men ; but it this is going on much longer, I for one shall resign, as I do not think it fair. Why can't they be given to the Committee themselves, and if they care to give them away let them do so, as it is the only thing they get far their services ; or give them to the players and let them give them to their friends. I have heard the players complain that they cannot get one for a friend,—Yours FAIRPLAY. Runcorn, N)v. 7, 1900.

===== 0002601_19001109_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Canon Maitland Wood and St. Michael's Bazaar.

Sir,—" One of the Ignorant " must indeed be most ignorant. for in his very unfair and unkind lett,r of aturday last he imputes to Canon Maitland Wood words which he certainly never said. I was standing next to the vicar on the platform at the time and I wish most emphatically to assert that he sever describetl the people of Runcorn as ignorant. It is a deliborate falsehold to say that he did, and the writer, if a gentleman, will retract a:. once. The speech was a humorous one, suitable to the open- ing of a tazaar,and even if in any jocular sense the words in question had actually been spoken it would be most unf air and unusual to treat them as serious but as a matter of fact they were never used at all. As to our lack of riches, it is evident that Runcorn is not a town inhabited by merchant princes or generous millionaires, and therefore we as Church people in com- mon with our Nonconformist friends are quite justified in going afield to invite wealthy outsiders to opera our bazaars. This is all the Vicar said on the point. No man has the interests of the town of Runcorn more at heart than Canon Maitland Wood, and no one has taken a deeper interest in its prosperity and success;— take for example his work in connection with the Tech- nical Institute—and that the people of Runcorn appre- ciate this interest and work, they have more than once shown in no unmistakable manner, and doubtless will again if necessary in spite of the disgraceful personal attack of one who has not the ceurage to sign his own name.—Yours truly,

W. U. STABLES.

Runcorn, November Bth, 1900.

POMPEII.-THE BURIED Cll Y.

On Thursday the Rev _Alfred Wells lectured in the

Brunswick Assembly Hall to a very large audience on "Pompeii : The Buried City." illustrating where he went and what he saw with 60 very beautiful views shown by the Oxy-Hydrogen lime light which was admirably worked by Mr J T Pickthatl. The chair was taken by Samuel Taylor Esq., J.P., who briefly introduced the lecturer: Toe first pic- ture shown was an admirab!e likeness of the Chairman which evoked great applause. The lecturer began with a description of the surround- ings of this city. In graphic terms he referred to Naples as "the city of fair women "and " bad smells" The ride to Pompeii from Naples with the lovely bay on one side, and marvellous vegetation on the other, was tersely described. and then the lecturer proceeded to speak of the city itself. He first gave an account of its history, which reached to the mythical period. The first record of the city was dated B.C. 310,but its monuments clearly proved it to have been of much greater ant,quity At the time of its destruction it was a city of some 30,000 inhabitants, and its situation was almost unequalled. Upon the verge of the sea, at the entrance to a fertile plain, on the banks of a navigable river, it united the convenience of a commercial town, with the security of a Et:lllßa' station, and the romantic beauty of a spot celebratad in all ages for its pre-eminent loveliness.—Then followed a most graphic and interest- ing account of its destruction in August, A.D.. 79, of its forgotten sta'e through long centuries. Of its first dic - covery in 1748, brought abeut by a peasant sinkinr. a well to find water for his garden—of the unintermit- tent attempts at exhuming, and of the steady process since followed. The mode of excavation was also described. The lecturer then proceeded to conduct his audience through the city describing its temples, • theatres, amphilleatre, basilica, forum, baths, streets, • steppiog-stones, houses, shops, monuments, museum. bronzes and frescoes, and illustrating these with 60 magnificent views. Mr. Wells dwelt upon the ,loatbsome character of the place, and pointed the moral • that Pompeii's sin caused Pompeii's destmction, closing - a powerful (Inf.-leen:Ant of the truth with some well- - known lines from Matthew Arnold. The lecture was in- • tensely interesting and alike entertaining and instruc- tive.—A vote of thanks to the Chairman closed the pro- , ceedieffq.

LATEST NEWS.

The Canadian Election. Increased Liberal Majority.

TORONTO, THIIRSULY.

Complete returns up to midnight show that the Liberals have captured 127 seats and the Conserva- tives 81. Returns have yet to come from one con- stituency and four elections are still to be held. The Liberals have a majority of 45. It is expected that the Liberals will be returned with a majority of 52,the largest the party has ever obtained. The result is regarded as a significant answer to the attempts to excite race antipathy letween the English and French Canadians.

The County Cr;nket

Championst.gp.

Proposed Alteration in Scoring.

Considerable dissatisfaction having been felt with the existing system of scoring for the county championship, Mr W E Dennison, at the rr quest of the M.C,C.. Cricket Sub—com- mittee, propounded a scheme which he has now submitted for consideration. By it it is proposed to attach certain value to drawn games, instead of entirely ignoring them as at. present. Briefly speaking, two points are to be reckoned for each win ; in the event cf a drawn game no count shall be taken of a match. unless the side going in first completes an innings. Should this be done the umpires at the close of the game shall adjudicate, and if they consider the game approximately even each side shall be credited with one point; if, on the other hand, one team bad obtained such a material advantage as to render victory probable urder a con- tinuation of existing conditions, that side shall be credited with one point. Either side may appeal to the M.C.C. committee against the umpires, decision, and the county whose total points bear the highest proportion to its possible maximum shall be champion county.

THE WAR.

How Captain Plomer was Killed. Letter from a Widnes Man.

Patrick MlSanus, a private in the Ist King's (Liver- pool Regiment) writing from Machadodorp,on September 28, 1900, to a cousin in Widnes, says:—l am pleased to inform you that lain still in the land of the living. We have had a terrible hard time since we left Ingogo on thi3 31;t ;,*1 July, 1 have beeo tbreugh three hard battles during the madth of A.l2lltt,ltiti haVo had quite enough of the war. At Machadodorp,from tud 2.3 rd to the Bth we had an exceptionally bard task, and one in Which great slaughter took place. The C Company, which I was in, went on the field 117 strong, and were en- gaged from 1.30 in the afternoon until dark. At the roll call at midnight forty-seven of our brave comrades were amongst the killed, wounded, and miss- ing. Captain Plomer,in.command of the company, was shot through the head, and the regiment had over 100 casualties in this same battle. I.believe the Boet. army is now broken up, but marauding bands,the remnants of commandos, still harass our positions. Lieutenant Dalt, a Reserve man, and myself were the last to leave he field, not being able to retire with the rest on account of the heavy Boer fire. They tried to steal upon us,but two or three volleys soon put them to the ri7ht-lhour. They were-frightened to face the music. Taey came again and asked us to surrender, but I (Pat) wasn't having any. It looks as though I have been through one world and come into another. I expect to he home to spend the C'nristrnas in Widnes, as our commander has sent in all the names of the Resnve men who are to come home.

HOW THE WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS ARE FARING.

===== 0002601_19001109_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
News from Captain Appleton. ---- Hopes to be Home at Christmas. ---- Death of Private F. Woods.

The following additional letters have been received from Captain Appleton. who ie in charge of the first hatch of Warrington Volunteer who !eft for active service in South Africa. nay are addreesed to his Wet.- chip the Mayor :—Vry held, September 28th, 1900 Dear Mr. Roberts.—l am vain enough te imagine that you will like to hear of us again, and of our movements since leaving Gras Kop, and so I am writing in time for the next Englieh mail. All our detachments near Sand- spruit were relieved by the Queen'e %Vest Surrey Reei ment, and our Brigade cencentra'ked at Sandepruit with a large force of Mounted Infantry and guns, under Generals Hildyard and Wynne, for a second advance on Wakkerstroom and other towns. We reached the former after two days' trek, ard with very slight opposition. The guns with the advance guard and the Naval 4-7 fired a few shots, and the Mounted Infantry were engaged and several poor fel- lows shot. But they brought in some few prisoners and shot some of the enemy. We were close to the town at this time, and could go into it every day. It is a pretty town, with the usual fine stone Dutch Reformed Church, and all the peaches being in full bloom. There are orchards of them round nearly every house, as there are round all the farms, and contrasted with the fresh green of other trees they had a very pre'Ay effect. Oranges—and generally very sweet ones—are to be had. They are grown near here in great quantities, and we gathered some more lemons on the way here. There are some English people in all these towns, and they seem to have heel a rather rough time of it when the Boers re- turned after our last visit. After staying a few days, and leaving a garrison we went on again to Utrecht ever some most magnificent country, and through and down some splendid passes. We must have gone down some thousands of feet before reaching Utrecht and the change in the temperature was at once apparent.and the heat during the day rather trying at first. Utrecht is also a pretty little town, but the people were rather sullen, and not inclined to sell 114 much at first. They thought we should eat their babies, as they had heard we terrible English were doing this, and they appar- ently believed it. Their ignorance is astounding, and they are naturally fed up with all sorts of lies. They t elieve that the Transvaal flag has appeared on the sun, and that a supernatural being, in the form of a whi.e horse, has appeared to give them victory. The English people tell us that they firmly believe this. They were told here that our dead were lying in heaps at Botha' Pans and that we had lost some 2000, men between Wakkerstroom and here, We left a garrison at Utrecht, and arrived here about a week ago. This is a fair sized town, and is an interesting and rather aa imnnrtant place,. It was formerly, of course, part of Zululand, and the Boeis established a new Republic here, which was only amalgamated with the Transvaal a few years aim. I have been shown a photoraph of their old coat-oe-aeme, which differs from the Trammel one. Rorke'a Drift is only some 30 miles away. The veldt is getting quite preen, altheugh we have only had two days rain, and ,wers are epringing up everywhere, and ate very pretty. We found lots of maidenhair fern among the rocks. lam sorry to say I have lost another Warring- ton man. Poor Private F Woods died on the 15h. at Howich, from enteric. after a very long illness. He was a very good fellow, and a well-knewn Orford Harrier. He was one of our mess waiters at Warrington for some time. I know how s,rry you would be to hear of his death Rumour says we are going to Dundee from here, but I do not know whether it is true. There is a railway almost completed to Dundee, in fact it only re quires the girder bridges and the metals, and can be completed in a few weeks. I bhould imagine that there will always be a strong garri- son here, as it is so near Z rluland. I met a Baptist Minister at Wakkerstroom (the Rev. T. R. Matthews) who very kindly procured for me a "Committal to prison document," containing the signatures of Kruger and Leyds, and sent it on to me. lam sending it to my sister by this mail. After Colonel Ridgway and our officere have seen it, it will he sent nn to you. It may he of some interest at this time,end I shall be very glad if eon will show it to any Warrington people who care to cee it. Will yen kindly return it to my sister when you have done with it? I get a Warrington paper every, week, se we get to know a good deal of what is gc- ing on in the old town. I suppose you will have a General Election on soon, and, of course, you will be preparing for the municipal elections now,—With kind regard from Major Adam, and the same from myself to you and Mrs. Roberts, and hoping to see you again before Christmas.

"P.S.—The Boors are on the hills at A safe distance from u.. They only snipe at our mounted patrols, and sometimes at our outposts. There is a good deal of urine from farm. flying the white flag, and the farmg are then promptly bl-own up. It is a bard necessity. but they do not appreciate more lenient treatment, aud their treachery is appalling." "Sunday, September 30th. The English mail arrived yesterday, and was, es usual, very welcome, though a very sad one to several of us, as it told us of the sudden death of Mrs. Eloieson. One can hardly realize that her large heart will net bo there to welcome us when we get home. I have one or two of her clams in my

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, NOVEMBER 9 1900. NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

company, to whom she constantly wrote, and we have, indeed, lost a dear friend in her, as have many people in Warrington and elsewhere. 1 am sorry to say I have still a good many sick in the company, but no serious cases as far as I know. We only number about 72 now fit for duty, and, as you know, many are back in England again. The draft under Carey laas never reacned me, but has been pounced upon for duty near Harri- smith. I am not sure whether I ever acknowledged your second letter, if not please accept now my hearty thanks for it, and all its welcome news. It was very good of you to find time to write again among all your numerous duties and engagements. The cycle parade and football sports were successful. The troops have played a cricket match against Vrvheid and beaten them, and our regiment has played Bethune's Mounted Infantry and beaten them. Both our teams were cap- tained by Major Adam." Another Letter from Police Constable Howard. Hard Times with the Guards.

Below is a copy of a letter received by a member of the Warrington Police Force. from Police Constable Samuel Howard. a Guards Reserveman, now serving with his regiment, the 3rd Grenadiers, in the Transvaal. P.C. Howard, it will be recollectei, along with PC. Hurst, was called away from the force in October, 1899, to join his regiment for active service in Snith Africa. He joined Methuen's column with the brigade of Guards, taking part in Belmont and succeeding battles, the cap- ture of Cronje, and the advance on Bloemfontein, Johannestarrg, Pretoria and Komati Be has thus remained in the fighting line for a whole year, As no news had been heard of him for several months the pre- sent letter has been heartily welcomed by the members of the force, who are, naturally, greatly pleased to hear that their gallant comrade is still safe and well : " 3rd Grenadier duards, Pretoria October 4th, 1900 Dear comrade.—Just a few lines in answer to your. latter. We have had a hard time of it since I wrote to you last. We had a grand reception at Johannesburg and also at Pretoria. We next marched on Diamond Hill, and had a hard fight with the enemy, after which we retired towards Pretoria, to a place called Doncker- hoek. After lying there for about three weeks we marched on Komati Poort with Pole Carew. We en gaged the enemy at Belfast, arid fought for four days. The Boers must have had a heavy list of casualities, as we were all round them. They charged a battery of our guns, but did not live to get back. If they had stuck to their position for another day perhaps Botha wenld have had the pleasure of seeing Cronje; but he ran away with his defeated army,and we have been pursuing him ever since. I think they (the Beers) are ab4ut done for. We had a very mountainous country to deal with between Belfast and Komati Poort. One day we had reveille at three a.m. and marched off at five a.m. We had a drop of coffee starting off, but nothing to eat. We marched about 23 miles that day, end when we arrived in camp it was late at night. Tne first news we heard when we halted we that the water was about a mile dirtant, so, rather than walk another couple of miles,we did without it. We were then served out with a cupful of flour,and had that not been obtained by capture from the Boers we should have Ina nothing to eat all day. As we had no means of cooking it most of the men threw it away. We lay down on the splendid (!) piece of ground chosen for the camp; it was covered with rocks and stones,l, having one of the latter for a pillow. We slept till next morning without a thing to cover us except the thin khaki with which we are clad. When we woke up after about three hours sleep we were sur- prised to find it daylight, ad wondered no preparations were being made for another day's march, as we were supposed to do 19 miles that day. A rumour Ulm got about the camp that the melical officers would not allow the division to go any farther that day, as five men had died on the march the day previous. The same day 36 mules dropped dead. Its a goad job we did not proceed that day, or half the men would have been ruined. As it was I felt as though I should have fallen, had anyone touched me. It took us about 14 days to march from Waterval Onder to Komati Poort. It was rather a long time, owing to the rough country. and we had ta go a good way round on accouet of the transprt not being able to climb the hills.We also had for many a long mile,to cut our way through the bush, where we saw plenty of monkeys jumping about in the trees When we arrived at Komati Poort, we found that the Boers had fled, and left the town nothing bat a burning mass. We marched through the railway station and found that a great many locomotives had been left behind, though mast of them were crippled. There was nothing left of the trucks and carriages but the red-hot iron frames. We bivouacked on the !banks of the Crocodile River, on the opposite side of which we could see the Portuguese sentries. We fired all one day at a hippopotamus ; every time he put his nose out of the water a volley Was sent at him, but it took no effect. We are now at Pretoria, after about three days by rail, in cattle trucks, from Komati Poort. Every time we came to an incline we bad to jump out and push up behind. We very often have to help the mules up a hill with the transport waggons, but this last" puts the top hat on." I eeern to be very lucky. The train that followed us, with the Coldatreams, had the lines blown up in front They were attacked by the Boers, and say. they had 20 casualties. I shall he glad when this is all over and we are on our way to England.but I think that the end is not far distant, and we hope to be home by Christmas, This is all I have to say at present. Trust- ing all my comrades on the force are doing well and in good health, as:l am at present, and with best wishes to all, I remain, sincerely yours. SAMUEL HOWARD."

TRESPASSING ON THE LONDON AND NORTH-VirEsTERN RAIL- WAY AT RARLESTOWL—FINES INFLICTED.—SamueI Apple- ton and John Blower were summoned for trespassing on the London and North-Western Railway, at Rarlestown on the 14th October, in such a manner as to expose themselves to danger or risk of danger.—Mr Fenna prosecuted on behalf of the railway company and stated that the proceedings were taken under a special Alt of Parliament entitled the London and North-Western Rahway additional Pow ,rs Act, 1833. which rendered the defendants liable to a penalty of 4Ce. A great deal of trespassing had teen going on recently. The men crossed over the Haydock colliery line and proceeded across the triangular piece of land to the Vulcan foundry and Messre Slee's works. PROPOSED PUBLIC 'CLOCK IN Sr. PETER'S CHURCH TOWER.

At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee, the cloairmaa read a letter which he had received from the Rev. James Ryder, vicar, and Messrs. James Liptrott and W. El. Flinaley, wardens, of St. Peter's, Newton-in- Makerfietd, asking the Council to take into consideration the question of placing a clock in the church tower, and of illuminating aiid keeping the same in proper order. Ic was proposed by Mr. T. Ormand, aod seconded by Mr. W. Gilmour, that, subject to the confirmation of the Council, a four-`aced clock be provided and fixed in the church _tower, at a cost of not exceeding £lOO, and that the clock when so fixed b 3 lighted and maintained at the cost of the Council ; tho clock for ever afterwardi to remain the property of the Council; also that the chair- man of this committee and Councillors T. Ormand anti A. Smith, with the clerk, be appointe 1 a deputation to confer with the Tower Committee.

Mr. Gilmour pointed out twit the resolution referred to a four-faced clock, whereas he understood it was the intention of the church authorities to only provide a three-facd clock. It would look ridiculous for the C )uncil to approve of a four-faced clock when it was only intended to have a threc-faced clock. Mr Bennett inquired whether the Council were justi led in putting a clock in a church tower. The Chairman said the committee was very careful to investigate its legal position before coming to a decision It was clearly laid dawn that the C)uncil had power to purchase a clock for such a purpose. Mr Gilmour said they had a lawyer present at the time. (Laughter.) He thought the Council was justi- fied in provialing a public clock for Newton on the ground that the Newton people had paid their quota towards the clock in the Town Hall buildings at E arles- town. Be thought it would tend to the peace of the neighbourhood. Mr Orrnand proceeded to read the section of the Public Health Act dealing with the question, and which empowered the Council to spend public money on clocks in church towers. It was under that section that the General Purposes Committee moved the resolution. It was thought that a clock with three or four dials might be orderei at a cost not exceeding £lOO. Mr. Gilmour said he had only introduced the subject with the object of preventing friction. Mr. Ormancl ?aid it would be necessary to obtain a faculty from an ecclesiastical court, but he took it that the Ecclesiastical power would be granted before the committee came before the Council and asked for prc- vision to place the clock in the tower. The Chairman said all these matters were gone into by the committee. He was sorry Mr. Emmett was not present. Mr. Emmett said from the reading of the Act of Parliament there appeared to be some doubt as to their power to place a clock in a church tower All dQubt ought to be removed • before they pas ed such a resolu- tion.

Mr• Blrron said it was quite clear from the reading of the Act that the Council had power to put in a clock. It was for the Ecclesiastical authorities to object to it, so far as he could see. So long as they did not object he thought the Council might give the sam3 facility to Newton as they had given Co Eirlestown,at a very much lrgr covl than was asked on this occasion. A public clock at Newton would be of great benefit to that dis- trict.

Mr. Emmett said he was not opposing the erection of a clock, but he wanted to be satisfied that the Council was in order, and he iid not think the Cocnqil would be in order until the Ecclesiastical power had been obt‘ined.

The Chairman thought the sub-committee appointed might he safely left to deal with the matter. Mr. Simpson said the Rev James Ryder, together wizt, the wardens had made the appicatim on behalf of St. Peter's Church, and they would naturally see that the faculty wa forthcoming at the proper time.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] LIOENSING REFORM,

BY THE HON. SIDNEY PEEL.

Introductory.

There is no domestic question of such vital impor- ance as the problem of the drink traffic. There is hardly any social question of the day into which the consumption of intoxicants does not enter in some form or another. If we are considering measures for the prevention of crime, we find that drunken- ness and drinking are its most prolific causes; if we are considering the amendment of the poor- laws, drink as a factor in the creation of poverty is not less striking. The evils of drunkenness both to the individual and his family are too well— known to need emphasising, but it must be borne in mind that drunkenness and its attendant conse— quence are not the only, and perhaps not the worst mischief arising from the consumption of liquor. Without ever being drunk a man may spend upon liquor a vastly greater proportion of his income than he is entitled to, when his own needs, and still more those of his family are taken into consideration.

It has ben calculated that tbe average weekly expenditure of a working-class family on intoxi- cants is about 6s. Many families do not spend any- thing like so much, but of course as many spend a good deal more. When the general rate of wages is taken into account, any one can see what an exces- sive expenditure takes place Without taking an extreme view of the effects of alcohol, it is easy to understand that this excessive expenditure means so much deduction from the amount spent' on the food, clothing, education and amusements of the family, with a correspondingi loss of general effici- ency to the nation, which s perfectly appalling The advantages which a teetotal family possesses over its drinking,but not drunken neighbours seems almost unfair;

The problem then is how to diminish this exces- sive consumption of alcoholic liquors. There are two methods of effecting this object, one by impos- ing legal restrictions upon the facilities for °brain- ing intoxicants, the other by employing all the forces of moral *suasion and education, and by endeavouring t ) improve those conditions which lead to drink, for instance, improvement of the dwellings of the poor, and by providing counter attractions to the public-house and the social activi- ties now so much represented by and bound up with the consumption of liquor. There can be little doubt that both methods should go hand in hand The drinking customs of the country are too deeply rooted for either to be able to combat them successfully alone. Drink leads to poverty,crime and bad conditions of living; poverty, crime and bad conditions of living lead to drink ; and this vicious circle will never be thoroughly broken unless it is stoutly assaulted from every side. I only propose, however, in these articles to deal with the method of restricting by law the facilities for obtaining liquor. I shall examine the present licensing system, its results and the amendments of it which are desirable. I shall discuss the more extreme proposals for the direct popular control of the liquor traffic, and those municipalisation or local management schemes, which aim at combining both sides of temperance reform. And first I shall endeavour to describe as concisely as possible the main features of the pre- sent law. OUTLINE OF THE PRESENT LICENSING LAWS

Hardly any branch of law bearing directly on practical life and administered by laymen is so complicated and so hard to find in detail as the law ' relating to the sale of intoxicants. It is scattered up and down tie statute book; one of the most important and governing Acts dates from 1828, and there must be about 250 Acts of Parliament now in force dealing wholly or in part with the subject. This complexity of the la„wis a very seriou obstacle to its good administration, and the need for simpli- fication and codification is very urgent. Such a codification is not, however, likely to be undertaken by Parliament, unless it is accompanied by a cora- prehen.sive reform. But while the law is complicated in detail, its main features are clear enough. Every person wishing to sell intoxicants, has to obtain a license from the Ex ise Authorities, and pay a certain duty according to the nature of the license. But in the case of nearly all retail licenses, the excise license cannot be obtained except on the production of a certificate or license from the Licensing Authority The Licensing Authority consists of the justices of the peace. They annually hold a general licensing meeting for the purpose of granting licenses, which do not continue for more than a year. They have, with some exceptions, fall power to refuse any license applied fo- for the first time, but in case of a refusal to grant a license to premise 3 which have been licensed the previous year, there is an appeal against their decision to the quarter-sessions of the county. The duties of the licensing authority are not con- fined to the annual licensing meeting; the justices may between whiles transfer a license from one holder to another, and they may also grant per- mission (called an occasional license) for a publimn to sell liquor elsewhere than on his premises, as well as extensions of the hours of sale on special occasions.

There are various kinds of licensed houses selling intoxicants by retail, some for consumption both on and off the premises, others for consumption off only. The "on ' houses are principally public- houses, or fully-licensed houses, where all kinds of intoxicants are sold, and beer-houses for the sale of beer only. Of " off" -licenses the most important are those under which beer is sold, and those under which spirits are sold. The so-called grocers' license in England,:which any shopkeeper may take out, is really a combination of several licenses, generally to sell in bottles.

The powers, which the licensing authority have, i differ n the case of the different kinds of licenses. There is full discretion to ref use for any reason that may seem good,public-house licenses,all beerh.ouses, which have been granted for the first time since May, 1869, and all off-licenses under which beer is sold : the discretion is limited in the case of beer-houses which have been licensed contin- uously since before May, 1869, and in the case of off-licenses under which spirits or wine are sold. Thus while the justices, as licensing authority, have very important functions, the excise author- ties have a purely mechanical duty to perform in granting licenses, viz, to see that the proper duties are paid, and that the justices' license is produced when this is necessary; they have no discretion to refuse a license, if the necessary preliminaries have been complied with.. . _ _ _

A third set of officiqls are concerned in the ad- ministration and enforcement of the liquor laws, the police. It is their duty to deal with drunken persons, to supervise the conduct of licensed houses to prosecute breaches of the law, and to report to the licensing authority. Among those important regulations, which have been laid down by law for the conduct, of licensed houses,are those relating to the hours during which no sale is permitted, which will be dealt with more in detail at a later stage, and the prohibitions against permitting drunken— ness, serving drunken persons, ha-bouring pros- titutes, and harbouring or serving policemen while on duty.

THE /DEAL CONTEMPLATED BY THE LAW

Such a bare outline of the licensing law can naturally give but little idea of its spirit. Before we come to the actual state of things, it is well to consider what the law aims at. The licensing authority ought to grant just so many licenses as may be demanded by the needs of the district, with which they are supposed to be intimately acquainted The license holders ought to be specially selected persons in a position re- moved from the stress of competition, and so able to live without unduly pushing their sale, either by permitting drunkenipss or by harbouring improper persons Unfortunately, the actual state of things is widely different. Almost everywhere the number of licen- sed houses is utterly excessive beyond any possible theory of the requirements of the neighbourhood. There has also grown up behind the law the develop- ment known as the tied house system. While, in spite of the careful inquiry that is supposed to be made into the character and position of the license- holders, it must be confa9sed, without any wish to disparage the publicans as a class, that they are in no degree superior to their unselected neighbours. In spite of the fact that the law forbids drunken- ness to be permitted in the public-house, a very large number of drunken persons are annually gathered from the streets, and a much larger could be gathered were it not the usual practice of the police to leave those who are not so drunk as to be a danger to themselves or the public. We will proceed to examine into the effects of the excessive number of licensed houses, and the tied- house system, and then inquire into the cause of what can only be described as the failure of the

===== 0002601_19001109_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
present licensing laws. THE EXCESSIVE N1711,,8ER OF ,LICENSED HOUSES AND

ITS EFFECT. The proportion of all licensed premises to popu- lation in England and Wales is 1 to about 230. If it, is remembered that the number 230 includes child- ren, women and many others who for :various reasons consume little or no intoxicating liquors, and that there are of course some new districts, which are not over-licensed, it will be seen at a glance how great the excess must be. There are 15 boroughs and 15 petty sessional divisions, where the proportion is 1 to under 100, some going down es low as 1 to 60; there are 65 boroughs and 114 petty sessional divisions where the proportion is between Ito 100 and Ito 150. Even in districts where the general proportion of licensed houses is less, par- ticular parts may be very much over-licensed. Thus in Portsmouth the average is 1 to 153. In Portsmouth parish there are 25 licenses as compared with 31 food shops. In the High Street 1 house 14 is licensed, in one street as many as 1 in 5 The worst case is on the "Hard," where though trade has shifted and what necessity there may have ones been no longer exists,l3 out of 27 houses in a space of 191 yards are licensed, and licenses are thicklY strewn among the neighbouring streets and narrow disreputable alleys The locality is known as "Tile Devil's Acre"

Many other instances might be quoted; especiallY in some villages, in counties near London, the con- gestion of licenses is very extraordinary. At first sight it would seem to be obvious that the more places for the sale of drink the more drinking. but some people are inclined to argue that a reduc• tion of licensed houses would have no effect on the consumption. They do not, however, usually press the argument to its logical conclusion, namely, that in case the number was reduced the brewer Bhoul4 receive no compensation, because his sales of beer are not diminished They rely chiefly on police statistics of drunkenness, which can be obtained such a form as to appear to shew that a number ol places with a smaller number of licensed houses have more drunkenness than places with a greater number. Such calculations arenot worth the pert they are written upon In the first place, polies, statistics are useless as a criterion of the amount drunkenness in a place. They depend upon a nual; 'her of varying circums ances ; the strength an.' activity of the police force, the number of lock-P5 to which drunken men can be taken, the regulations in force for the treatment of drunken persons,and s° on. For instance, in Brighton the number of arrest's for druekenness in one year was observed to be nearly double those of the preceding years ; the el: planation was not an increase of drunkenness, WI: an increase of police activity owing to the app ment of a new chief constable. Secondly, other statistics can be produced showing an opposite state of things. Thirdly, no one asserts that mere twin; bet of public houses is the only factor in producint drunkenness ; the size and position of the house: ma have a good deal to do with it. Again a Ole', with few public houses may have often calve it' present tending to promote drinking, e g., big wages, climatic conditions, particular forms of eat: ployment ; so that it is impossible to compare, s' is usually attempted in such calculations, a bustling Northern town, where wages are high el! police active, with some Southern county,where people are very poor, and the police few and fa between. There is then nothing to refute the opinion that if you reduce the number of houses, you will reduce drinking and drunkenness, by the mere reduction of facilities and removal of , temptations. Other considerations point to the same conclusion First, the excessive number renders the cotnPe'l tion among the publicans very severe. It is often hard to make a living, and the publican is forced tn.„. resort, if not to illegal practices, to disreputahl! ones. He is not inclined to scrutinise the conditio.i's . of a customer too closely, for he knows that 111; neighbour will serve him if he does not. He givhe, sweets to children to attract them, and. gives " long pull," or more than a pint for a pint' A price: he gives free concerts and doubtful entertainment; m his premises. Secondly, the number wake' proper and efficient police supervision a practios impossibility. Thirdly, each publican has probabl a regular cliente!e of personal followers, who vilL drink for the good of the house, and the more limns there are, the larger is this class ; th more persons there are actively engaged in puzli',"4 the sales in their own interests, the, larger the 836' are like:y to be. There can be little doubt, when all this is takells into account, tat the number of licensed hoiise.s a factor of the first importance in encouraV,"F' drinkinw and drunkenness. There is an iairliedi4V° and crying necessity for a large r,,duecioe in tb 7,1 numbers. At a later stage 'we sh 1" all diBeB ' methods of effecing this reduction, as svail as tile I question of compenation, which is bound oP,,itll nl i it. We hare now to consider the other reer"n the development of the present administration of t I licensing laws, the tied house system.
